The caller (`select_from_garden()`) already tolerates a missing category via `.get(category, 0.5)`, so `_category_weights()` was fixed to build its scoring dict from whatever categories actually appear in the data (`scores.setdefault(category, [])`) instead of a fixed pre-declared enum — verified both in isolation (mixed `dream`/`ai_tech`/`general` categories score without error) and live (the same restart that previously errored every 10-40s ran multiple clean `[SCHEDULER] Selected: ...` cycles with zero recurrence).
- **Batch 6 (memory & sync correctness):** `memory_bridge.py`'s `retrieve_relevant_memories(source_filter=...)` fell back to the *unfiltered* top-k whenever fewer than top-k tagged entries existed — a caller explicitly filtering to exclude autonomous self-talk could still get exactly what it filtered out mixed back in; now returns whatever matches the filter, even if that's fewer than requested, rather than padding with excluded entries. `conversation_service.py`'s `non_autonomous` fallback used `!= "autonomous"`, which trivially passed for any untagged/legacy entry (`None != "autonomous"` is `True`) — untagged content bypassed the exclusion this fallback exists to enforce; now requires an explicit non-`None`/non-empty/non-`"autonomous"` tag (verified: untagged and autonomous-tagged entries excluded, real `user_conversation`/`claude_research` entries pass). `sync_protocol.py`'s `export_since()` had no per-line exception isolation — a single interaction-log entry with an explicit JSON `null` (not missing) `"timestamp"` or `"quality_score"` crashed the whole function (`.get(key, default)` only substitutes for a missing key, not a present `null`; `float(None)` raises), deterministically breaking cross-machine sync until someone found the offending line; now wrapped per-entry with null-coalescing on both fields. `memory_write_validator.py`'s `check_empty_or_trivial()` flagged an empty reflection/dream as `severity="warn"` rather than `"block"` — despite the function's own docstring saying "block entries with no meaningful content" — so a generation failure producing empty output still got vector-committed as a real memory; now blocks (verified: empty reflection is correctly rejected, `EMPTY_SIGNAL`'s existing block behavior unchanged). `run_sync_cycle()` advanced the sync cursor unconditionally after the push loop, even when a batch failed partway through (an exception, or a non-200 response that was previously silently skipped with no log line at all) — every remaining batch's entries then fell before the new cursor on the next cycle, permanently excluded from ever being retried; now only advances when every batch actually succeeds, logging `status: partial_failure` otherwise (verified live via a mocked 500 response: cursor correctly stays unchanged).
- **Batch 7 (entry points, desktop app & routes):** Echo Studio's `_ChatStreamWorker.run()` never surfaced any signal if the server-side SSE generator died after streaming tokens but before a final "done" frame — `api_client.stream_chat()`'s loop just ends with no exception in that case, so neither `done` nor `error` fired and the UI stayed stuck (composer disabled, status never clearing); now tracks whether "done" was actually seen and emits `error` if the stream ended without it. `DualLearner.start_training()`'s "already running?" check was unlocked (a real TOCTOU race given `/start_training` has no auth — see Batch 0), and `_train_loop()` had no exception handling at all — a real failure mode exists where the TF-IDF/hash embedding fallbacks produce a different dimensionality than `TinyModel(emb_dim)` was built for, silently killing the daemon thread with the caller having already received `{"started": true}`; both fixed (lock added, training body wrapped with `logging.error(exc_info=True)`). `local_store.py`'s multi-statement SQLite methods (`add_message` + `touch_conversation`'s separate commit, `delete_conversation`'s three DELETEs, `record_recent_file`'s two statements) had no shared transaction boundary — a mid-method failure left a dangling uncommitted write on the connection until some later, unrelated commit flushed it in; all three now use `with self._conn:` (commits together or rolls back together). `markdown_view.py` auto-opened any clicked link via `setOpenExternalLinks(True)` — content here can come from memory/file/model-generated text, including content potentially influenced by the pre-Batch-0 unauthenticated write endpoints, so one crafted link was one click from opening via the OS handler; now requires explicit confirmation. `echo_json_server.py`'s `/response` route cleared the outbox file immediately after reading it, before the HTTP response was even built — a dropped connection between those two steps lost queued messages irrecoverably; now clears only via `after_this_request`, after the response has been handed off.
- **Batch 8 (state/safety + Low-severity cleanup):** `introspection_channel.py`'s `_collect_system_health()` defaulted `ram_pressure_pct` to `0.0` on total `psutil` failure — indistinguishable from a genuinely idle system to `system_guard.py`'s throttle, the one consumer this reading exists for; now writes an explicit `None` on total failure, and `system_guard.py` treats that specific sentinel as should-throttle=`True` (fail closed) rather than a healthy reading. Fixing this surfaced a real regression risk caught before it shipped: `echo_state.py`'s dim[3] computation read the same field with `float(sh.get("ram_pressure_pct", 50.0))`, which would have raised `TypeError` on the new `None` sentinel (the default only applies when the key is *missing*, not present-as-null) — fixed alongside the same pass. `echo_tool_context.py`'s path-root guards used bare `startswith(_PROJECT_ROOT)` (no trailing separator), the same class of gap already fixed correctly one file over in `echo_tool_dispatch.py`; both call sites brought in line. `echo_ground_truth.py` used `se.get(...) or intr_se.get(...)` for "hours since last self-edit," which discarded a genuine fresh `0.0` (falsy) in favor of a possibly-stale fallback — fixed with an explicit `is None` check. `modelfile_proposer.py` only had an insert-if-absent fallback for `temperature`, not `num_ctx`/`num_keep` — a proposal targeting either of those against a Modelfile that didn't already declare them silently produced a byte-identical file while still reporting success; both now insert correctly (verified). `council_rater.py`'s `_poll_and_rate()` saved its cursor immediately after slicing a new batch, before processing any of it — a mid-batch failure permanently skipped every remaining entry in that batch; moved to after the loop. `echo_cartographer.py` had five separate issues: `sorted(project_root.rglob("*.py"))` eagerly materialized the whole generator, so one permission-denied subdirectory aborted the entire scan with zero output — switched to `os.walk(onerror=...)`, which also fixes the second issue (symlinks were followed unconditionally, risking unbounded recursion on a self-referential link — `os.walk`'s `followlinks=False` default closes this); `modules.module_name` (a bare filename stem) was the SQLite primary key, so a dead archived file sharing a stem with a live one (confirmed real: `archive_janitor/bible_injection.py` vs. the live protected file) silently overwrote it via `INSERT OR REPLACE` — `path` is now the primary key (verified: both files' rows survive a scan); `update_runtime_hits()`'s SQL was syntactically invalid (multiplying a FROM-clause subquery directly, which SQLite can't parse) — confirmed to raise on any real execution, rewritten as a plain scalar subquery (verified); the stdlib-noise import filter used `startswith()` against prefix strings, incorrectly dropping real local modules that merely start with the same letters (`reflection_shard`, `system_guard`, `sentence_transformer_singleton`, `logging_setup` were all confirmed real hits) — switched to exact-match on the first dotted component. `sandbox/logging_setup.py`'s handler setup ran once at import time only; `runner.py`'s `close_handlers()` (called in a `finally:` after every sandbox run) closed and removed them with nothing to ever re-add them, so sandbox logging went dark after the first run of every server session — every `log_*` wrapper now re-establishes handlers if missing (verified: handlers correctly return after a close). `sandbox/run_script.py` textually prepended an `input()`-disabling shim before the *entire* script, breaking any script starting with `from __future__ import ...` (which Python requires to be the first statement) — now AST-aware, inserting after any leading docstring/future-imports (verified: previously-breaking case now parses cleanly). `spot_check.py`'s `_write_back()` trusted a raw line index captured once at the start of the run with no verification it still pointed at the right entry, and had no guard against overwriting an already-rated entry (unlike `council_rater.py`'s equivalent, which already guards this) — now verifies by `source_timestamp` before writing, falls back to a full scan if the index has drifted, and refuses to overwrite an existing rating (all three cases verified). `project_learner.py`'s AST parse failures were silently unlogged, and `read_text()` used `errors="ignore"` (silently dropping non-UTF8 bytes rather than making them visible) — both fixed. `dual_learning.py`'s `INDEX_PATH`/`self.index_path` were genuinely dead (never read anywhere — confirmed via grep, unlike `META_PATH`/`self.meta_path`, which are real and in active use for event-count tracking) — removed; a non-context-managed `open()` in `list_recent()` was also fixed to use `with`.
  - **Not individually fixed, left as documented low-priority items** (per the original plan's own framing — independently trivial, batched for awareness rather than action): `self_model_updater.py`'s excluded `autonomous_*` task-type list from targeting eligibility; stale comments in `river_creative_rehab.py`; ~~`echo_core.py`'s un-isolated event bus~~ (superseded 2026-07-15 — see the Machine-Native Awareness section's Global Workspace entry: this was more than "un-isolated," it was 100% dormant, zero callers anywhere; now hardened and has real publishers); `terminal_client.py`'s single-thread-only session-history assumption; Echo Studio's silent-drop-on-in-flight-refresh across 4 dashboard views; `wolf_friction_bridge.py`'s cursor ordering (re-checked directly — this file's actual code already saves the cursor after its parse loop, not before, and has zero live callers anyway, so the finding as originally written doesn't apply here); `sandbox/run_script.py`'s unisolated dynamic-execution path (a real, narrower-than-first-feared gap — confirmed reachable only via a manual CLI flag today, not any automated path — documented here rather than patched, since a proper fix means porting the real F2 Seatbelt-profile sandbox approach, a bigger job than this pass).

**Finding 23 — `app.mlx_handler` self-edit candidates permanently fail F2 via transformers→torch→`ctypes.CDLL`; a pre-import fix was prototyped, then reverted after it was shown to reopen a native-code-execution bypass (investigated and rejected 2026-07-12):** Overnight self-edit monitoring (post-Finding-7-era sandbox `sys.path` fix) surfaced a new, consistent `staging_import_failed` pattern for any candidate doing `import app.mlx_handler`: the import chain is `app.mlx_handler → mlx_lm → transformers` (transformers unconditionally imports `torch` during its own module init, regardless of whether torch tensors are ever used) `→ torch.__init__.py`'s own `ctypes.CDLL(...)` call to preload MKL/CUDA-adjacent shared libs — a routine, benign thing torch does on every normal, non-sandboxed import too. F2's wrapper (`sandbox/safe_exec_wrapper.py`) blocks `ctypes.CDLL`/`_ctypes.dlopen` unconditionally, so this trips every time, before any of the candidate's own logic runs. A fix was prototyped mirroring the wrapper's existing `ctypes`/`cffi` "pre-import before patching" pattern: pre-import `torch`/`transformers`/`mlx_lm` in Phase 1 (before write/ctypes-blocking patches install), so a later `import torch` — candidate or transitive — just hits the module cache instead of re-triggering `_load_global_deps()`. **This was reverted before shipping.** A direct empirical test (simulating Phase 1 pre-import followed by the same Phase 3 `ctypes.CDLL` block) found that `torch.ops.load_library(<arbitrary path>)` — a real, documented torch API for loading custom native `.so`/`.dylib` extensions — sailed straight past the patched `ctypes.CDLL` and reached genuine OS-level library loading (confirmed: failed only because the test path didn't exist, not because it was blocked). Root cause: torch captures its own reference to the real `ctypes.CDLL` at torch's *own* import time; patching the `ctypes` module's `CDLL` attribute afterward doesn't touch torch's already-bound copy. Pre-importing torch would therefore hand any self-edit candidate — LLM-generated, unreviewed, exactly what F1/F2 exist to constrain, and demonstrably prone to hallucination all night (`import_hallucination`/`safety_blocked` entries throughout `memory/SELF_EDIT.log`) — a standing, permanent way to load arbitrary native code, not just a theoretical one requiring deliberate malice. The benefit being traded for that (avoiding a handful of foreseeable, cleanly-logged `staging_import_failed` results per hour) was judged not worth the cost. **Decision: leave `app.mlx_handler` imports as a permanent, accepted dead end for self-edit candidates**, in the same spirit as the other documented dead-ends in this file (empty directories, orphaned files) — not a bug to keep re-investigating. `sandbox/safe_exec_wrapper.py` is unchanged from its Finding-7-era `sys.path` fix; no new pre-import code was merged. Separately confirmed during the same investigation, restricted to real source (88 live `.py` files, excluding historical `self_edit_plans`/backup/staging text that inflates naive greps): Echo's own code directly imports 9 packages with native/C extensions (`numpy`, `faiss`, `torch`, `scipy`, `scikit-learn`, `sentence-transformers`, `psutil`, `tiktoken`, `mlx_lm`), plus 4 more pulled in only transitively (`transformers`, `tokenizers`, `safetensors`, `sentencepiece`). `numpy`/`faiss`/`scipy`/`scikit-learn` never hit this same wall, before or after — they resolve native BLAS/LAPACK deps through the OS's own dynamic linker during ordinary C-extension loading, not via a self-called `ctypes.CDLL()` the way `torch` does, so this specific collateral-blocking class is narrow to `torch` (and whatever sits downstream of it).

**Finding 24 — Neither sandbox pipeline scopes GUI/display access; LLM-generated code can open a real on-screen window (found 2026-07-13, flagged, fix deferred — Gremlin's call):** Reported directly by Gremlin: an unexplained window occasionally flashes open and vanishes almost instantly while Echo is running, on both M5 and the 2020 Intel MacBook ("Ark"). Confirmed root cause, not a system quirk or hardware issue: `sandbox/experiment_runner.py`'s `_SAFETY_HEADER` (the safety wrapper prepended to every LLM-generated "computational experiment" before it runs) blocks exactly two things — writes outside `sandbox/` and network access (stubs `socket`/`urllib`/`requests`/`httpx`/`ftplib`/`smtplib`). It does not block GUI toolkit imports at all. One real generated experiment confirmed on disk, `sandbox/experiments/exp_20260706_004311.py`, does `import matplotlib.pyplot as plt; plt.scatter(...); plt.show()` — `matplotlib` is installed here with the interactive `macosx` backend (confirmed via `matplotlib.get_backend()`), not the headless `Agg` backend. Run inside a short-lived, 60s-timeout sandboxed subprocess rather than matplotlib's expected main-thread/event-loop context, this plausibly explains the "flashes and vanishes instantly" symptom (the window's event loop likely never gets pumped before the process moves on or is torn down) rather than a normal window that blocks until closed. Reproducible on both machines because it's the same shared pipeline behavior, not anything machine-specific. Rare in practice: only 1 of 52 experiment files currently on disk shows this pattern (~2%).

Checked whether this also reaches the higher-stakes self-edit F2 kernel sandbox (`sandbox/echo_sandbox.sb`) — structurally, the same class of gap exists there too, though **no occurrence has been observed or confirmed on that path**. The profile's `(allow mach-lookup)` line — documented as required for normal Python/ObjC runtime internals (objc bridge, CoreFoundation) — is the same Mach IPC channel that reaches the WindowServer; the profile was never scoped to deny display access, only filesystem writes and network (its own header comment confirms this is the intended, documented boundary). F1's AST scanner (`scan_for_unsafe_operations()`) has no concept of a "GUI-triggering import" as a blocked pattern — it blocks specific dangerous operations and paths, not specific real-but-risky library imports like `matplotlib`, so nothing today would stop a self-edit candidate from doing the same thing.

**Priority assessment (Medium — not Critical/High, not Low), reasoning recorded rather than just a label:** Does not defeat the actual boundary F1/F2/F3 and `_SAFETY_HEADER` exist to enforce (filesystem write and network isolation both remain fully intact) — no data exfiltration, no persistence, no privilege escalation demonstrated or plausible from `mach-lookup` alone (screen *capture* or accessibility control are separately gated by macOS's TCC consent layer, which would itself surface a visible system permission prompt, not something covert). Weighed against that: it's a real, confirmed, reproducible gap in a security-relevant boundary that nobody had previously scoped or documented, it's user-visible and reasonably alarming to witness unexplained on a machine running autonomous code, and it's structurally present on the self-edit path too even though unconfirmed there. Net: worth fixing, not urgent.

**Decision: flagged, not fixed — deferred pending Gremlin's call**, same posture as Finding 23's documented dead-end, but here the fix itself is simple and low-risk if/when it's wanted: extend `_SAFETY_HEADER`'s existing network-module-stubbing pattern to also neutralize GUI toolkits (`matplotlib.use("Agg")` forced before any experiment code runs, plus stubbing `tkinter`/`PyQt5`/`PySide6`/`wx` the same way `socket` etc. are stubbed today) — and consider the analogous change for the self-edit F1/F2 path for consistency, lower urgency there given zero confirmed occurrences. Not applied in this pass.

**Correction (2026-07-15, Finding 28): the sandbox infrastructure underneath this finding has since changed, though the underlying gap this finding describes was not re-verified either way.** `sandbox/experiment_runner.py`'s `run_experiment()` no longer runs generated code in a bare subprocess plus `_SAFETY_HEADER` as described above — it now routes through `run_sandbox_script_isolated()` (`sandbox_interface.py`), the same kernel-level `sandbox-exec` + `echo_sandbox.sb` isolation the F2 self-edit pipeline uses, unifying what were three previously-independent unisolated runners (`sandbox/runner.py` — since removed and consolidated — `experiment_runner.py`, and `sandbox_loop`'s script runner). Whether this incidentally changed the matplotlib/GUI-window exposure this finding describes was not re-tested in this pass — the fix target (`_SAFETY_HEADER` and `echo_sandbox.sb`'s `allow mach-lookup`) may no longer be the exact right place to intervene given the infrastructure has moved; worth a live re-test before acting on the fix sketch above as originally written.

**Fixed 2026-07-15, at the corrected location.** `sandbox/safe_exec_wrapper.py`'s `_install_patches()` — the actual shared enforcement point for both the F2 self-edit path and every sandboxed script/experiment run — now sets `MPLBACKEND=Agg` in the environment before any candidate code runs (matplotlib reads this at import time, more reliable than patching `matplotlib.use()`) and stubs `tkinter`/`_tkinter`/`PyQt5`/`PyQt6`/`PySide2`/`PySide6`/`wx` via `sys.modules.setdefault(name, None)`, the same mechanism already used for network modules. `_SAFETY_HEADER` in `experiment_runner.py` got the same two additions for defense-in-depth consistency with its own stated (now secondary) role. Verified two ways: (1) a real matplotlib-importing, `.show()`-calling script run through `run_sandbox_script_isolated()` failed before ever reaching `.show()` — matplotlib can't create its config/cache directory at all inside the write-restricted sandbox, an incidental but total mitigation that predates this specific fix (a side effect of the kernel-level Seatbelt migration Finding 30 already documented); (2) a script that only inspects environment/`sys.modules` (avoiding matplotlib's unrelated config-dir failure) confirmed directly: `MPLBACKEND` reads `Agg` inside the sandbox, and `import tkinter` raises `ImportError: import of tkinter halted; None in sys.modules`, exactly as intended.

**Finding 25 — Two independent watchers checking the same self_model.json claim, with tolerance logic that had already started to drift apart from itself (found and fixed 2026-07-13):** `self_report_verifier.py`'s `verify_memory_health()` (called every 60s from `dmn_guardian.py`'s guardian loop) and `liveness_ledger.py`'s `self_model_drift` check (called every 120s from `introspection_channel.py`, added earlier the same session) were independently recomputing the identical comparison — self_model.json's claimed `faiss_vector_count`/`journal_line_count` against fresh ground truth — via two separately-maintained code paths that could, and had already started to, disagree on how strict to be. `self_report_verifier.py`'s tolerances were better-reasoned than the ledger's original ones: a relative 5%-of-claimed (floor 50) tolerance for FAISS (confirmed live: it can grow by hundreds of vectors in the couple of minutes between refresh cycles from autonomous loops constantly writing memory, so a tight tolerance would fire constantly on benign lag) versus a tight flat 5-line tolerance for the journal (any real gap there likely means a genuine bug, not lag — this whole module's own motivating case was exactly a journal-count bug, introspection reading the wrong file). The ledger's original tolerance used a flat 2%-of-current-value rule for both fields, looser on the exact field (journal) where looseness matters most.

Consolidated rather than just deleted one: migrated `self_report_verifier.py`'s tolerance reasoning into `liveness_ledger.py`'s `self_model_drift` check (now the single source of truth for this comparison), then retired the redundant call site in `dmn_guardian.py`'s guardian loop — the ledger's version already covered everything `self_report_verifier.py` did and adds a staleness check (self_model.json age vs. a 24h threshold) the older module never had, surfaced through the standard `/admin/liveness-status` channel instead of a separate log file. `self_report_verifier.py`'s functions are left in place, not deleted — only the live call site was removed — in case they're wanted for something else later, same posture as `self_heal.py`'s intentional disconnection. Verified, not assumed: re-ran the full discrimination test suite after the tolerance change (all 27 cases still pass, no regression), confirmed the old call site is fully gone from `dmn_guardian.py`, and ran the updated check live against real current data — correctly flagged a genuine current drift (self_model.json's cached `faiss_vector_count` predates the FAISS split-brain migration documented above and hadn't refreshed yet), proving the consolidated check is actually catching real drift, not just passing its own test suite.

**Finding 26 — `propose_core_edit()`: a real, safety-correct, tested advisory feature with zero live callers (found 2026-07-14, flagged, not wired — Gremlin's call):**

**Update (2026-07-15, Emergence roadmap Phase 1, Area 6):** wired, deliberately as a manual-only tool per Gremlin's explicit decision — not auto-firing on a forbidden-target hallucination. `terminal_client.py` now has a `!propose <target_file> <prompt>` command (`request_core_edit_proposal()`, mirroring `!edit`'s existing pattern exactly) that calls `propose_core_edit()` directly. No new autonomous trigger exists anywhere — this makes the mechanism reachable by a human who already has a specific protected-file change in mind, nothing more. Verified live, both paths: a non-forbidden target correctly returns the existing rejection message ("X is not a protected target..."); a real proposal against `app/core/self_model_updater.py` produced `self_edit_proposals/20260715205006_app_core_self_model_updater_py.patch` with a genuine, split council verdict (2/3 APPROVE, `llama3.2:3b` dissenting with real substantive reasoning, not a rubber stamp) and a real unified diff, headed `NOT APPLIED. Never auto-applied. For human review only.` `self_edit_manager.py` contains a fully-built function, `propose_core_edit(target_file, prompt, intensity)`, specifically for the case a normal self-edit can never handle: a candidate that would target a file in `EDIT_FORBIDDEN_TARGETS`. Read directly and confirmed the docstring is accurate, not aspirational — it never calls `save_code()` and never writes to `target_file`; it reads the real current contents, generates a plan, asks for a minimal unified diff (not a full-file replacement — a code comment explains why: `query_ollama()`'s payload hardcodes `num_predict=512`, so a full-file request against any real production file reliably truncates mid-file), runs the diff through `_council_review_core_edit()` (a genuine multi-model advisory review, explicitly documented as never gating the write either way), and writes the result to `self_edit_proposals/*.patch` — headed with the target file, a timestamp, the originating prompt, the safety scan note, and every council model's individual verdict — for a human to read and manually apply if they agree. This is a careful design, not a bypass: `EDIT_FORBIDDEN_TARGETS`'s enforcement (lines 215, 728) is completely independent of this function and unweakened by its existence.

**It is currently unreachable.** `grep -rn "propose_core_edit(" --include="*.py" .` outside its own definition returns zero hits — not called from the autonomous self-edit loop, not exposed via any admin route, not wired into `terminal_client.py`, nothing. The only evidence it has ever run: three `SELF_EDIT.log` entries, all from 2026-07-13T02:21–02:38 UTC (before this finding's session started), all targeting `app/core/introspection_channel.py`, each with a real recorded council verdict (`0/3 APPROVE`, then `3/3 APPROVE`, then `2/2 APPROVE`) — genuine council activity, not placeholder text. A nearby code comment ("during testing, before `propose_core_edit()` switched to requesting a diff...") confirms this was exercised by hand while the function was being built, not by anything autonomous. `self_edit_proposals/` exists but is currently empty — whatever `.patch` files that testing session produced are already gone (manually cleared or pruned; `_prune_stale_proposals()` only tracks `.patch`-suffixed files and two of the three historical log lines show a `.py`-suffixed path instead, a naming inconsistency from that same testing period worth knowing about if this is ever revived, though not chased further here).

**Decision: flagged, not wired — deferred pending Gremlin's call**, same posture as Finding 23 and 24. Unlike those two, there's no known safety cost to connecting it — the open question is purely design (should it fire automatically whenever autonomous generation happens to hallucinate a forbidden target, or stay a deliberately manual/on-demand tool someone invokes when they actually want a proposal for a specific protected-file change) — which is exactly the kind of default-by-momentum decision this doc's own findings keep warning against making by omission. Not applied in this pass.

**Finding 27 — `memory_write_validator.py`'s module-level log-file open is structurally incompatible with the F2 sandbox; any self-edit candidate importing `memory_bridge` is guaranteed to fail (found 2026-07-14, flagged, not fixed):** Surfaced as a direct side effect of fixing Finding 26's neighbor — a sandbox error-truncation bug (see the `[:400]`/`_extract_sandbox_failure_text()` fix committed the same day) was quietly hiding this. Once real tracebacks stopped getting cut off, the actual failure behind a large class of `staging_import_failed` results became visible: `memory_write_validator.py:107` runs `log = _get_validator_logger()` unconditionally at **module level** — not lazily, not behind a try/except — which calls `logging.FileHandler(VALIDATOR_LOG_PATH)` (`memory/validator_audit.log`, a real production path). Reproduced live: any self-edit candidate that does `from app.core.memory_bridge import <anything>` (which imports `memory_write_validator` at `memory_bridge.py:28`) hits `safe_exec_wrapper.py`'s write-blocking patch the instant that `FileHandler` tries to open its log file, raising `PermissionError: [SANDBOX] Write blocked outside scratch dir: '.../memory/validator_audit.log'` — **before the candidate's own code ever executes**, regardless of whether that code is correct. This is deterministic, not racy: every sandbox test is a fresh subprocess, so `_get_validator_logger()` re-runs and re-fails every single time.

**Scope, measured not guessed**: 244 of 1282 archived self-edit candidates (`sandbox/scripts/archive/`, ~19%) directly `import app.core.memory_bridge`. **Update (2026-07-15, Finding 30): `sandbox/scripts/archive/` now holds 3,517 files, up from 1,282 the day before — nearly tripled in roughly 24 hours.** The 244/1282 ratio wasn't re-measured against the new total, but the accumulation rate itself is worth noting: this is consistent with (and plausible evidence for) Finding 27's own memory_bridge-import wall still generating a steady stream of `staging_import_failed` results, still unfixed as of this update. That's a floor, not a ceiling — anything importing `app.lib.vector_memory`, `app.core.memory_tools`, or any other `memory_write_validator` caller hits the same wall via a different transitive path, uncounted here. No historical log entry can confirm how many past `staging_import_failed` results share this exact cause, precisely because the truncation bug this finding was found underneath was hiding the evidence — that's a real, acknowledged limit on how far back this can be attributed, not glossed over.

**Not fixed in this pass, deliberately** — this touches `memory_write_validator.py`, a module used by the live write-gating path for every real memory write in the system (`add_to_vector_memory`, `log_dream_bridge`, and everything that calls them), not an isolated self-edit-only file. Plausible directions, not evaluated against each other here: make `_get_validator_logger()` lazy and failure-tolerant (catch the `PermissionError`/any open failure and fall back to a no-op logger rather than crashing the whole import chain); special-case the sandbox to tolerate this one known log path; or accept it and steer self-edit candidates away from `memory_bridge` imports they don't structurally need. Each has different blast radius and none were chosen — flagged for Gremlin's review, same discipline as Findings 23/24/26, not defaulted into by momentum while the fix that revealed it was already in flight. **Still unfixed as of Finding 28 (2026-07-15) — re-confirmed live, not just re-asserted: `log = _get_validator_logger()` still runs unconditionally at `memory_write_validator.py:107`.**

**Fixed 2026-07-15:** `_get_validator_logger()`'s `logging.FileHandler(VALIDATOR_LOG_PATH)` open is now wrapped in try/except; on any failure (the sandbox's `PermissionError` included) it falls back to `logging.NullHandler()` instead of letting the exception propagate and kill the whole import chain. Normal (non-sandboxed) operation is unaffected — the real file write already succeeded there before, and still does. Verified with a direct test mocking `logging.FileHandler` to raise `PermissionError`: the module now imports cleanly and `log.info()` calls succeed silently via the null handler, instead of the import itself failing.

**Finding 28 — Full-repo verification pass: five parallel research agents plus direct checks, run specifically to test whether this doc's own claims still hold (2026-07-15).** Prompted by a request to build genuine expertise in the codebase rather than trust CLAUDE.md's word for its own state — the exercise doubled as a real test of this file's accuracy. Every numbered correction elsewhere in this document dated 2026-07-15 came out of this pass; this entry is the consolidated summary plus the findings that didn't fit as an inline correction to an existing section. Nothing below was fabricated or inferred — each item is cited to a direct source read, a live log/JSON file, or an independently-run check.

- **`apply_to_code` is deployed and 253/254 invocations fail — but the liveness ledger's own check for this still reports `pass: true`.** Full detail folded into the Self-Edit System section above (search "not_deployed above is stale"). This is the single most important finding of the pass: it demonstrates that a check built correctly against this doc's own "verify against ground truth, not self-report" principle can still mislead, because *evaluation criteria* (here, a 7-day window with no notion of a recent-failure streak) is a separate failure mode from *fake evidence*. `_evaluate_apply_to_code()` in `liveness_ledger.py` needs a "how many of the last N invocations succeeded," not just "did any invocation ever succeed in the window" — not fixed in this pass, flagged for the next ledger-hardening round.
- **`ModelGuidedOrchestrator`'s hourly loop (`echo_model_guided_orchestrator.py` → `app/core/autonomous_loop_with_optuna.py`) has zero throttle/stillness gating.** Confirmed by direct read and by `autonomy_coordinator.py`'s own docstring, which names only three loops it protects (`emergent_loop`, `autonomous_loop.autonomous_loop`, `self_edit_loop`) — this one isn't among them, and nothing else in the file gates it either. It runs its full fetch+sandbox+Optuna cycle every hour regardless of RAM pressure or stillness state. Compounding this: `model_guided_autonomous_loop()` and the `autonomous_loop_iteration()` function it calls each maintain their *own independent* `OPTUNA_SLEEP=7200` timer against the same module-level `EchoOptuna` singleton, with no lock — both timers are seeded at 0, so they can plausibly evaluate true in the same pass and fire two separate 10-trial Optuna batches (one with `param_hints`, one without) back to back, on top of `run.py`'s separate hourly `AutonomousSelfEdit` thread doing its own real `perform_self_edit(dry_run=False)` call. Not previously documented; not fixed in this pass.

**Fixed 2026-07-15 (both pieces):** `model_guided_autonomous_loop()` now calls `should_run_cycle("model_guided_orchestrator")` at the top of its loop, same pattern as the other three autonomy loops (skip → log → `time.sleep(120)` → `continue`) — it is now covered by `autonomy_coordinator.py`'s shared gate and will appear in `GET /admin/autonomy-status`. The double-fire was fixed by consolidating both independent timers into one: `autonomous_loop_with_optuna.py` now exposes `try_run_optuna(param_hints=None)`, which atomically checks-and-updates the shared `_cycle_state["last_optuna_run"]` under a new `_optuna_gate_lock` before calling `optimizer.optimize_self_edit()`. Both `autonomous_loop_iteration()`'s internal call and `model_guided_autonomous_loop()`'s hinted call now go through this single gated function instead of each keeping an independent timer — whichever caller's turn it is supplies its own `param_hints`, but only one can fire per `OPTUNA_SLEEP` window. Verified: both modules import cleanly and resolve to the identical `try_run_optuna` function object.
- **`ReflectionShardAutonomy`** (`app/subsystems/reflection_shard.py`, owned by `EchoCore`, started at `run.py:1092`) **is a real, live, previously entirely undocumented autonomous loop** — not mentioned anywhere in this file before now. 300s cadence, 35% chance per cycle to call `observe()`, which appends to `memory/reflection_journal.jsonl`. The live instance is `BecomingReflectionShard`, which additionally generates a synthetic `<<emergent-pattern>>`-tagged meta-reflection every 10th observation. Its "leak" mechanism (`_emit_fn`, gated at a further 3% chance + 600s cooldown) is fully implemented but wired with `emit_fn=None` in production — meaning despite the code supporting external emission, nothing it does is currently observable anywhere outside `memory/reflection_journal.jsonl`. Verified inert, not verified dangerous.
- **Sundry undocumented/stale-reference items, each independently confirmed:** `app/internet_tools/query_reflect.py` exists and was previously unmentioned (thin `echo_query_reflect(keyword)` helper). `RiverBrain`'s actual class definition lives in `echo_model_orchestrator.py`, not `river_deliberation.py`, despite the naming suggesting otherwise. `echo_quality_scorer.py` lives at the repo root, not under `app/core/`. `prompt_workspace.system_note()` gained an `own_record: bool` parameter since Finding 9 shipped, distinguishing externally-injected content from the system's own genuine track record (used by `echo_ground_truth.py`) — a real refinement never documented. `sandbox/runner.py` no longer exists; it was consolidated into `sandbox_interface.py`'s shared isolated-runner helper (see the Finding 24 correction above for what now uses it).
- **Methodology note, for future passes**: this was done via five parallel read-only research agents, each scoped to a subsystem and explicitly instructed to verify this doc's claims against current source rather than summarize them, plus direct manual verification (running the liveness ledger and its discrimination suite live, tracing `apply_to_code_invocations.jsonl` by hand, confirming thread-start call sites by grep) for the highest-stakes claims rather than trusting any single agent's report. Two agents independently arrived at the identical `apply_to_code` failure count (253/254) via separate methods, which is the kind of cross-check this doc has repeatedly said is the only thing that actually earns trust.

**Finding 29 — `/message/send` claims "local-only" in its own docstring but has zero authentication (found 2026-07-15; resolved 2026-07-15 — confirmed intentional, not a bug):** Gremlin confirmed directly: Echo instances should have open communication over Tailscale, so the missing auth on this route is the correct, intended design, not a gap to close. `app/routes_messaging.py`'s `message_send()` docstring — previously wrong, claiming "local-only" — was corrected to state plainly that this route is intentionally unauthenticated within the tailnet, same access-control model as the read-only `/admin/*` surface. No behavior change; the route was never actually local-only, only its docstring claimed that. Original finding text preserved below for the record. Unlike `/chat/stream`, `/chat/regenerate`, and `/download_model` — also unauthenticated, but framed by an in-code comment at `run.py:733` as a deliberate "Tailscale is the network boundary" design choice covering the whole `/admin/*` read-only surface — `/message/send` (`run.py:709-712` → `app/routes_messaging.py:76-88`) is mislabeled, not just unauthenticated by design. Its own docstring says "local-only," but it is a plain Flask route with no `_secret_ok()` call, and it triggers a real outbound delivery to the partner machine via `echo_messaging.send_message()`. Anyone reachable over Tailscale can make this machine send arbitrary messages to the sibling instance. Distinct from `/message/receive`, which correctly uses a separate `ECHO_PARTNER_SECRET` plus an origin allowlist (`_partner_secret_ok()`) — the inbound side is gated, the outbound-trigger side isn't. Not fixed here — same posture as Findings 23/24/26/27: flagged for Gremlin's call, not defaulted into a fix by momentum. Plausible direction, not chosen: gate `/message/send` with the existing `_secret_ok()`/`GREMLIN_SECRET` check the same way `/message/settings` already is.

**Finding 31 — `apply_to_code`'s documented "pure function" contract is unenforced; a real staged candidate smuggles unvalidated memory writes through it (found 2026-07-15, not yet fixed):** Discovered while live-observing the self-edit loop shortly after restarting the server post-Finding-28-fix (a genuinely fresh candidate, not a synthetic test case). One of several freshly-staged dry-run candidates (`staging/self_edit_candidate_929be7d1f2c54d7a81bed2210e2eadfc.py`) defines `apply_to_code` calling `app.core.memory_tools.append_memory_entry()`/`log_memory_edit()` — real functions that `open(path, "a")`-append to `memory/memory_journal.log` / `memory/self_edit_reflections.log`. Neither F1 (scans the candidate's own top-level code for dangerous operations; doesn't scan inside already-approved imports like `memory_tools`) nor the Finding-28 F2 smoke-test fix (calls `apply_to_code()` inside the sandbox, where the write would be denied — except `memory_tools.py`'s own functions wrap their write in a bare `try/except Exception` that silently logs and swallows any error, including the sandbox's `PermissionError`, never re-raising) catches this. Confirmed directly against `_apply_self_edit_output()` (`self_edit_manager.py:1427`): it calls `fn(code)` in-process, unsandboxed, in the real server process, and only records `changed`/`error` in `apply_to_code_invocations.jsonl` based on the *return value* — it has no visibility into side effects a candidate's code causes along the way. Practical effect if ever deployed: every real `apply_to_code()` invocation would write to those two files with model-generated content, unvalidated by `memory_write_validator.py` (a separate, simpler journal path, not the FAISS write gate), while the trusted invocation log — the same log `liveness_ledger.py`'s `self_edit_apply_to_code` check reads as its evidence — would show a clean, unremarkable `changed: false, error: null` entry, blind to what actually happened. **One thing checked and ruled out, not just assumed fine:** a different staged candidate defines `apply_to_code` as a class method instead of a module-level function; confirmed both `_apply_self_edit_output()`'s `getattr(module, "apply_to_code", None)` lookup and the Finding-28 F2 smoke-test use the identical module-level check, so a class-nested method is consistently invisible to both — inert either way, not a live gap. **Fixed 2026-07-15.** `_apply_self_edit_output()` now wraps the real `apply_to_code(code)` call in a new context manager, `_block_writes_for_apply_to_code()` (`self_edit_manager.py`) — a process-wide, lock-guarded, temporary patch of `builtins.open`, `io.open`, and `pathlib.Path.open` (the last one closes a gap the first draft of this fix missed: `Path.write_text`/`write_bytes` route through `Path.open`, not `builtins.open`, mirroring the same distinction `sandbox/safe_exec_wrapper.py` already draws) that denies all write-mode opens for the duration of the call and flags the attempt via a yielded dict — critically, the flag is set *before* raising, so it's still detected even when the callee's own exception handling swallows the resulting `PermissionError` (exactly what `memory_tools.py`'s functions do). If a write was attempted, `_apply_self_edit_output()` now rejects the candidate's output entirely (returns the original `code` unchanged, even though the hook itself didn't raise) and logs a new evidence value, `blocked_write_attempt: <file>:<line> in <function>`, into `apply_to_code_invocations.jsonl` — the same trusted log the liveness ledger reads — with the real call site captured via `traceback.extract_stack()` for debugging, not just a boolean.

Deliberately process-wide rather than scoped to the hook's own module namespace: verified directly that the vulnerable write happens inside `memory_tools.py`'s namespace, not `self_edit_generated`'s, so a per-module patch (setting `open` in just the hook's `__globals__`) would not have reached it — would require maintaining a fragile, silently-decaying allowlist of every module a hook might legitimately import instead. Known, accepted residual (same posture as F1/F2's own documented gaps): for the brief lock-guarded window this patch is active, an unrelated thread doing a genuinely legitimate write could theoretically collide and be blocked too; C extensions and `os.open`-level writes bypass a Python-level patch like this one entirely. Network calls and other non-file side effects are out of scope — this fix targets the specific, demonstrated gap (file writes hiding behind an approved import), not a general purity sandbox.

Verified against the real vulnerable candidate, not a synthetic reconstruction: replayed `929be7d1...`'s exact `apply_to_code` (including the real `app.core.memory_tools.append_memory_entry`/`log_memory_edit` calls) through `_block_writes_for_apply_to_code()` directly — confirmed zero bytes written to `memory/memory_journal.log` / `memory/self_edit_reflections.log` (byte-identical file sizes before/after), `attempted["write"]` correctly `True`, and the captured site correctly reading `memory_tools.py:40 in log_memory_edit` (the real call site, not the wrapper — an off-by-one in the stack-depth calculation was caught and fixed during this verification, not after). Also verified a genuinely pure candidate (leading-whitespace-stripping) passes through unflagged, and that ordinary reads (e.g. `open("CLAUDE.md")`) are unaffected by the block — confirming it isn't over-broad.

**Finding 32 — `prose_stripping`'s self-edit prompt was purely descriptive, no concrete example, likely the real reason 93+ cycles never converged (found and addressed 2026-07-15, Emergence roadmap Phase 3, hypothesis not proven):** `_FOCUS_FAMILY_BY_CREATIVITY`'s `prose_stripping` `domain_sentence` (`self_edit_manager.py:2237`) — the actual prompt text driving every one of those cycles — was read directly, not inferred: a natural-language task description ("strip any leading natural-language sentence before the first valid Python token") with zero concrete input/output example, zero edge-case specification. Consistent with 30+ genuinely different generated implementations across the historical record and, more concretely, with Finding 31's own candidate sample: three separate historical versions of `apply_to_code` (`is_prose`, `regex_pattern`, `re`) all failed on every real invocation with the same class of bug — a missing `import re` — because the prompt never told the generator to remember its imports. **Fixed, in the sense of "the identified gap was addressed," not in the sense of "convergence is now confirmed":** rewrote the sentence to add (1) one concrete before/after example pair, so the model has something to pattern-match against instead of inventing the transformation from an abstract description alone, (2) an explicit reminder to import every module used, directly targeting the recurring `NameError`, (3) an explicit statement of the purity constraint Finding 31's fix now enforces at call time, so future candidates aren't generated only to be silently rejected by the write-block after the fact. Read back the full rendered prompt via `_build_targeted_prompt('coding', 0.1)` to confirm it reads as coherent, well-formed prompt content, not just syntactically valid string concatenation. Deliberately did not reset `self_edit_convergence.json`'s existing `non_convergent_streak` for this family — doing so to make this fix's before/after comparison look better would be exactly the kind of self-report manipulation this whole ledger effort exists to catch. **What this cannot claim:** whether the rewrite actually improves convergence is unverified and unverifiable synthetically — it requires watching real subsequent cycles' `non_convergent_streak` and `all_names_seen` over time, not something this pass can close out on its own.

**Finding 33 — Liveness ledger and Global Workspace events were invisible in both Echo Studio and terminal_client.py; closed 2026-07-15.** A direct investigation (prompted by "check how our updates interact with Echo Studio") found `app/routes_echo_studio.py`'s `dashboard_health()` never called `get_liveness_status()` and `activity_log()` never read `memory/workspace_log.jsonl` — none of the 11 liveness checks or the Global Workspace events from Phase 2 (`world_model.surprise`, `dream.synthesis`, `self_edit.non_convergent`, `emergent_loop.salience`) surfaced anywhere in the desktop app, and terminal_client.py had no way to see them either. Chat grounding was already shared correctly (`_build_full_prompt()` calls the same `echo_ground_truth.py` used by `terminal_client.py`), and dream-cycle synthesis text already appeared in the memory browser and activity log via existing files — only the ledger and the workspace event stream were the gap. Fixed by extending, not replacing, the existing routes: `dashboard_health()` gained a `liveness` field (in-process call to `get_liveness_status()`, no HTTP/auth involved — same pattern as the existing `council`/`autonomy` fields) and `activity_log()` gained a `workspace_events` field (tails `memory/workspace_log.jsonl` the same way `council_recent` tails `council_ratings.jsonl`). No new endpoints were added, so no new auth surface. `echo_studio/views/health_dashboard_view.py` gained a 7th tile (`liveness: N/11`, red + tooltip listing failing subsystems when any check fails or the ledger is stale); `echo_studio/views/activity_view.py` gained a third splitter panel listing recent workspace events. `terminal_client.py` gained an on-demand `!status` command (direct in-process import of `get_liveness_status()`, same shape as the existing `!edit`/`!propose` commands — no HTTP round-trip needed since the terminal client already runs in the same Python environment as the Flask app). Verified live against the running server post-restart: `/dashboard/health`'s `liveness` field correctly showed 10/11 passing with `self_edit_apply_to_code` still flagged (consistent with the still-open Finding-28-era alert investigated the same day), and `/activity/log`'s `workspace_events` returned real recent `emergent_loop.salience`/`world_model.surprise` entries with genuine timestamps and salience values — not placeholder data.

**Finding 34 — Investigated every failure surfaced by the new liveness/workspace visibility (Finding 33), regardless of session of origin; one stale-evidence non-issue, one new-but-benign concurrency finding, two already-benign log lines (2026-07-15).** Prompted directly by seeing real `liveness`/`activity` data in Echo Studio and terminal_client.py for the first time — rather than let a flagged failure or a stray WARNING sit unexplained now that they're actually visible, each was traced to a concrete root cause.

- **`self_edit_apply_to_code` still FAILING — confirmed stale evidence, not a live bug.** The currently-deployed `apply_to_code` (`self_edit_generated.py`, file mtime 15:54, confirmed by direct read to have `import re` at the top — Finding 32's prompt fix took) has not been exercised by a single *real* invocation since redeploy: the last logged entry in `apply_to_code_invocations.jsonl` is `10:36:05`, over 5 hours earlier, from the previous, broken pre-fix version. The ledger's "20/20 recent invocations erroring" evidence is accurate but entirely pre-dates the fix — it will self-resolve the next time the self-edit loop generates a new candidate and the current hook actually runs against it. No action taken; nothing to fix.

- **New finding, real but low-severity: `Failed to persist VectorMemory data: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: '.../memory_meta.json.tmp' -> '.../memory_meta.json'`, 3x at this session's server startup.** Not previously documented. Traced thoroughly rather than assumed: read every one of `memory_bridge.py`'s 5 real `vector_memory.add()` call sites and confirmed all correctly wrap the call (and its internal `_persist()`) in `with memory_lock:`. Empirically stress-tested the real locked code path directly — 60 concurrent threads calling the actual production locking pattern against temp files (zero production data touched) — and got zero errors and a correct final count, proving the application's own locking logic is not the cause. Ruled out duplicate module instances (no bare, unqualified `memory_bridge` imports exist anywhere — every import is `app.core.`-qualified, so there's only ever one `memory_lock`/`vector_memory` singleton) and multiprocessing workers (no `Pool`/`Process`/`ProcessPoolExecutor` usage anywhere in the live codebase, despite a `multiprocessing.resource_tracker` process being visible in `ps aux` — that's an incidental side effect of a library's internals, not app code spawning workers). Found the real mechanism instead: `run.py` installs a custom `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT` handler (`shutdown_handler`, calls `sys.exit(0)`) — Python's interpreter shutdown is not instantaneous on a process this heavy (46k+-vector FAISS index, loaded ML models, many live threads), and the restart procedure used this session (`kill $(lsof -ti :5000); sleep 2; python run.py`) does not verify the old process has actually finished exiting before the new one starts. **Conclusion: a restart-timing artifact, not an application logic bug** — during the overlap window, the outgoing and incoming processes' background threads can both reach `_persist()` on the same fixed `.tmp` filename with no shared lock between them (they're separate OS processes; `threading.Lock` doesn't cross process boundaries). The exception is caught by `_persist()`'s own `try/except` (matches this file's "known residual gap" pattern elsewhere — fails loud in the log, doesn't crash anything) and self-corrects on the next `.add()` call from whichever process survives, since the in-memory `self.meta`/`self.index` the losing write was trying to persist still exists and gets re-attempted. **Deliberately left unfixed, per explicit instruction** ("it everything will balance its fine to leave it") — only happens during a manual restart, never during steady-state operation, and every plausible fix (waiting for the old process to fully exit before starting the new one, or making `_persist()`'s temp filename per-process-unique) was judged not worth the complexity for a self-correcting, exception-safe, restart-only edge case.

- **Two already-benign log lines, confirmed not to need any action:** a `[WARNING] load_project_map` line referencing a snapshot directory (`memory/snapshots/20260715T213430Z/`) that no longer exists on disk — confirmed it was rotated out by `snapshot_manager.py`'s 5-snapshot retention policy between being listed and being read, a self-resolving race already handled gracefully (logged and skipped, not a crash). And `ModelGuidedOrchestrator start_awareness_thread() called again — ignoring duplicate` — confirmed via the exact import-site comment in `echo_model_guided_orchestrator.py` ("imported from app.autonomous_awareness (singleton-guarded)") that this is the dedup guard working exactly as designed, not a new failure mode.

**Finding 30 — Full-repository sweep beyond app/core/: scaffold directories, archives, data stores, and root-level docs (2026-07-15).** Finding 28 covered the core subsystems (`app/core/`, `app/`, orchestration, sync). This pass covered everything Finding 28 didn't: every scaffold-sprawl directory, every archive/backup directory, `memory/`/`data/`/`staging/`/`sandbox/scripts/` contents, and every root-level `.md` doc. Method unchanged from Finding 28 (parallel read-only research agents per area, direct verification of anything safety-relevant or surprising before writing it down here — two agent claims were checked and one turned out to be a false alarm, see below).

- **A 17.2 GB quarantined file, `memory/reflection_journal_POISONED_20260607.jsonl`, was found with zero live code references anywhere** (confirmed via repo-wide grep) — genuinely inert, frozen since 2025-12-01, not previously mentioned in this doc despite its size. Its first line reveals what it actually is: an early, much larger-scale occurrence of the exact self-quoting pattern Finding 11 later fixed under a different mechanism (`reflect_on_knowledge()`) — a signal string reflected back into itself verbatim ("triggers these echoes: ..."). **Likely root cause of a separate, previously unexplained oddity**: this file's first line contains the literal substring `root:xnu-11417.140.69.701.11~1/RELEASE_X86_64` (from a logged `uname`-style OS signal) — almost certainly the source of the bizarre top-level scaffold directory `root:xnu-11417.140.69.705.2~1/RELEASE_X86_64/my_new_dir` (a slightly different kernel-build string, consistent with the same class of bug firing again later) — i.e., some code path was at some point splitting a signal/reflection string on `/` and using a fragment as a filesystem path. **Deleted 2026-07-15** after re-confirming zero live references and zero git tracking (untracked, so removal doesn't touch history) — 17GB reclaimed.

**Follow-up (2026-07-17): two more artifacts from this same incident found and deleted, discovered while investigating `memory/`'s real backup requirements.** `memory/archive/poisoned_20260607/` — a quarantine snapshot from the identical date and incident, containing a 168MB `faiss.index` and an empty (`{}`) `memory_meta.json` confirming it was already a dead reset artifact, not live data — same zero-live-references, zero-git-tracking profile as the file above. Deleted alongside it, in the same pass: `memory/archive/dream_bridge_bloat_20260614.log` (992MB), an unrelated but equally dead old operational log, rotated out and never referenced by any live code. Combined: `memory/` dropped from 2.0GB to 810MB.
- **`MIN_HEALTH_SCORE_TO_EDIT` (`app/core/config.py:7`) is real, fully-plumbed dead config.** It has env-override support (`os.getenv("MIN_HEALTH_SCORE_TO_EDIT", 80)`) but a repo-wide grep finds exactly one occurrence — its own definition line. Nothing reads it. The self-edit gate is, and as far as this doc can determine has only ever been, the hourly timer plus the cooldown described in the Self-Edit System section — there is no health-score gate despite a config variable existing that implies one. Worth knowing because the variable's existence could otherwise be mistaken for evidence such a gate exists. **Removed 2026-07-15** after re-confirming zero other references — rather than build the health-score gate its name implies, which nobody asked for.
- **`ECHO_READ_ONLY` (`run.py:79`, `os.environ.setdefault("ECHO_READ_ONLY", "true")`) is set unconditionally at startup but read by nothing else anywhere in the codebase** (confirmed via repo-wide grep, zero other hits). A variable named for a safety mode, defaulting to the safe value, doing nothing — this is worth flagging with slightly more weight than an ordinary dead-config finding, given the name specifically implies a write-protection mode that does not actually exist. **Removed 2026-07-15** after re-confirming zero other references.
- **`archive_janitor/` (Finding 6's territory) confirmed structurally inert, no security exposure**: all five modules Finding 6 names exist there, `archive_janitor/` is not on `sys.path` anywhere, and nothing else in the repo imports from it. No credentials found.
- **All 16 remaining scaffold-sprawl directories (`calculator/`, `chatbot/`, `new_directory/`, `PythonAnalysis/`, `NewBeginning/`, `MyPythonProject/`, `rebel_directory/`, `x86_64_env/`, `trash_autonomous/`, `feral_tools/`, `echo_scripts/`, plus four empty ones) were individually swept for secrets, not just assumed clean** — Finding 7 originally checked only `new_directory/`. Result: no new secret exposure found anywhere; the one real leak Finding 7 already found and remediated remains the only one. All 16 confirmed dead (no live `.py` imports from any of them) and confirmed as genuine self-edit-hallucination scaffolding on content inspection (near-duplicate `self_edit_backups/`/`self_edit_plans/` structure, generic/unrelated generated code between directories), not misidentified real projects.
- **`echo_json_server.py` (root) is a real, functioning, standalone Flask app — not Finding 6's dead SensoryHub, a separate thing entirely** — handling `/message`, `/response`, `/learning_event`, `/learning_batch`, `/ip` for what looks like a mobile/sensor companion client. It is not started by `run.py`, `start_echo.sh`, or any cron/plist. It binds the same port 5000 `run.py` uses; if ever run manually alongside a live `run.py`, they would conflict.
- **`verify_riverbrain.py` and `river_creative_rehab.py` (both root-level, both previously undocumented)** are legitimate manual diagnostic/rehab tools, not dead code and not live-autonomous — `verify_riverbrain.py` is notably well-built, dynamically regex-extracting and quoting `_do_save()`'s live source rather than hardcoding claims about it, so it stays accurate as the orchestrator changes.
- **`start_echo.sh` (root) is functionally the script `~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.gremlin.echo.plist` wants — just at the wrong path.** The plist points to `~/Scripts/start_echo.sh`; `~/Scripts/` exists but is empty. The repo-root copy does exactly what a correct launchd target would (conda activate, clear port 5000, crash-restart loop, logs to `memory/echo_watchdog.log`). Finding 8's "inert fossil" conclusion still holds (the plist doesn't work), but the fix would be as simple as repointing the plist or copying the script — not an "abandoned predecessor project," as Finding 8 might otherwise suggest.
- **`audits/2026-07-03/verify_claude_md.py` still runs today and is a genuine second opinion, independent of everything else this session**: 19/22 checks pass. The 3 failures are the already-known cooldown-reset claim (now corrected above) and two claims re-verified live and found to already be accurate (genesis-hash-at-startup — see the note below — and one unexamined further in this pass).
- **A claim from this same script was checked directly and found to be a false alarm, worth recording as a methodology note**: an agent this session reported the genesis-hash startup check "never appears in a startup path." Direct read of `run.py:972-992` shows it does — it's inline in `start_background_threads()`'s real startup sequence, and the surrounding comment confirms it was added specifically in response to this exact script's 2026-07-03 finding (audit C-2) that the check didn't exist yet. CLAUDE.md's Protected Files claim ("hash-verified at startup") is accurate. Recorded here specifically because it's a case of a sub-agent's finding being wrong and caught before being written down — the discipline this whole exercise depends on.
- **`EMERGENCE_ROADMAP.md`, `SELF_HEAL_PLAN.md`, `structure.md`, `HYBRID_MIGRATION_RESEARCH.md` are all aspirational/superseded design docs, not records of live functionality** — none of their proposals are implemented in current code (verified per-doc). `EchoStudio_Design.md` is the one exception: an accurate as-built doc matching the live, functional `echo_studio/` app confirmed in Finding 28's agent pass.
- **New findings from the sibling Claude instance's own briefings (`SIBLING_BRIEFING_FROM_ARK.MD`, `SIBLING_BRIEFING_FROM_PRIMARY.md`, both 2026-07-07, never folded into this doc):** Ark's fork has its own `ARK_MODE` throttle flag (confirmed via grep: does not exist in this M5 codebase, fork-specific, not applicable here) which was found to have the identical *shape* of gap as this session's `ModelGuidedOrchestrator` finding — a throttle gate covering one entry point (`run.py`) while a second, independent trigger path in `app/autonomous_loop.py` wasn't covered, caught via 83 rejected sandbox attempts over ~12 hours, since fixed on Ark's side. Worth remembering as a pattern, not a live M5 bug: whenever a new gate is added to guard an autonomous trigger, check every entry point that can reach it, not just the one being edited — this is the second time this exact shape of gap has been found in this project (once on each machine).

**Finding 35 — Emergence roadmap Phase 5/6/7.1 planning pass surfaced 14 bugs across the subsystems it touched; two fixed in the same pass, one retracted after verification, the rest deferred to a separate fix pass (2026-07-16).** Three parallel research agents investigating exact reuse patterns for the Phase 5/6 implementation plan (see the Machine-Native Awareness section above) turned up real, previously-undocumented gaps as a side effect — same discipline as Findings 28/30's own methodology, applied to a smaller, more targeted scope. Full list and severity in the implementation plan (`.claude/plans/sleepy-honking-yao.md`); highlights:

- **Fixed in the same pass:** dream-synthesis entries (`role: "synthesis"`) were escaping `_load_waking_memories()`'s "never resample dreams" exclusion, which only checked `role == "dream"` — one real unexcluded entry confirmed in `memory/memory_meta.json` before the fix (now also excludes `memory_source == "dream_v2"`). Stale check-count docstrings in `liveness_ledger.py`/`scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` ("eleven"/"nine") corrected to the true count.
- **Retracted after direct verification, not applied — the most important item on this list:** a planned fix assumed `shadow_model.propose()` had zero callers anywhere (based on a repo-wide grep) and proposed wiring it into `night_cycle.py`. Checked directly before applying, per this project's own standing discipline: `emergent_scheduler.py:620-625` already calls `propose_from_reflection()` on every real reflection cycle, and `memory/shadow_self_model.json`/`shadow_accuracy.jsonl` are genuinely live (1111 real entries, real `focus_matches` variation, last updated minutes before this was checked). The research grep had simply missed the call site. No code was changed — recorded here specifically because it's a case of a *plan's own premise* being wrong and caught before landing, not just an agent's prose summary.
- **Deferred, documented, not fixed:** `RiverBrain.model_task_stats["coding"]` is never updated by self-edit's own sandbox/dry-run quality signal (only by conversational quality scoring) — a latent mismatch-of-meaning risk if ever reused as a "predicted quality" baseline without noting this. `self_edit_outcomes.jsonl`'s 25-minute post-window is frequently empty (3 of the last 5 evaluated entries had `post: null`) — the pre/post delta signal is sparse in practice. The identical `min(rolling_10/5.0, 1.0)` world-surprise formula and `vec[1]` coherence-tension read were independently reimplemented three/two times respectively across `echo_core.py`, `emergent_scheduler.py`, `river_deliberation.py` before this pass deduplicated them onto `compute_salience()` (see Phase 6 above) — flagged here as the kind of drift risk worth watching for elsewhere in the codebase, not just this one instance.

**Follow-up (2026-07-17) — both deferred items above fixed for real, plus a third, separate bug the fix itself uncovered:**

- **The mismatch-of-meaning gap is closed with a genuinely separate signal, not a merge.** Added `"self_edit_coding": 5` to `TASK_TYPE_MAP` (`echo_model_orchestrator.py`) — same precedented pattern as "reasoning"'s own addition — giving RiverBrain a real, independent classifier/scaler/`model_task_stats` bucket for self-edit's own code generation, deliberately kept separate from `"coding"` rather than blended into it (self-edit candidate generation and conversational coding help are different skills; averaging them would have blurred a real distinction, not fixed one). `generate_code_from_plan()` (`self_edit_manager.py`, both the primary and retry attempts) now calls `river_brain.learn(model_name, "self_edit_coding", code)` on every real attempt, not just the ≤1/hour that reaches production — verified live: a real test call produced `model_task_stats={'self_edit_coding': {'count': 1, 'mean': 0.75}}`, a genuine 3/4 AST-based score, not a fallback value. `choose_model()`'s two self-edit call sites now pass `task_type="self_edit_coding"` instead of `"coding"` for their entropy/exploration signal. `echo_quality_scorer.py`'s two `task_type == "coding"` dispatch points were extended to `task_type in ("coding", "self_edit_coding")` so the new bucket still gets scored by the real AST-complexity logic, not a nonsense text-scoring fallback. **Honest limitation, found during verification, not smoothed over:** `choose_model()`'s *primary* ranking still comes from `rank_models()` — a separate, reflection-log-based mechanism that never reads `model_task_stats` at all. This fix corrects the entropy/exploration signal and gives self-edit a real, clean quality history for the first time, but it does not change what actually drives the top pick — that's a further, separate question, not addressed here.
- **The sparse post-window is fixed with a genuinely denser evidence source, kept as its own field.** `self_edit_outcome_tracker.py` gained a `dry_run_quality` field (pre/post/delta, same shape as `quality_score`) sourced from Optuna's `self_edit.dry_run_quality_delta` workspace events (Phase 7.1) — these fire roughly 10x/hour versus real conversational coding chats, which is the actual reason the old window was empty 6 of the last 10 times, re-confirmed live before this fix (worse than the "3 of 5" originally cited). Deliberately a separate field, not blended into `quality_score` — dry-run trial quality and real conversational quality are different measurements, same reasoning as the RiverBrain bucket above.
- **Building this surfaced a third, genuinely separate bug, found only because the fix was tested against real data instead of trusted on design:** `echo_core.py`'s `_log_workspace_event()` — the single shared logger every `publish_salience()` call writes through — built its persisted entry from a hardcoded field list that never included `detail`, silently dropping it on *every* write, for *every* publisher that ever attached one, since Phase 2a. Not something this pass introduced; something it found by trying to read `detail` back off disk, which nothing had ever done before (`self_edit_outcome_tracker.py`'s new dry-run-quality window is the first real consumer). Fixed at the root — the logger now persists `detail` — verified directly with a simulated event round-tripping correctly. Honest caveat: the 98 real `self_edit.dry_run_quality_delta` events already on disk predate the fix and have no `detail` to recover; new ones populate correctly going forward, not retroactively.

**Finding 36 — Fresh forensic audit (2026-07-16, `audits/forensic/`, supersedes the 2026-07-04 one in place) found `/mirror_echo` has no authentication, and follow-up with Gremlin surfaced a second, deeper bug in the phone symbiote client's own auth — both flagged, neither fixed yet:**

- **`/mirror_echo` (`run.py:485-520`), the route's own comment labels it the "MAIN ECHO ENTRY POINT," has zero auth of any kind** — no `_secret_ok()` call anywhere in the handler, unlike every comparable POST endpoint (`/nuke`, `/admin/restore`, `/inject_memory`, `/force_nightcycle`, `/learning_event`, `/learning_batch`, `/start_training`, `/memory/conversation`, `/sync/import`), all fixed with a shared secret check in the 2026-07-08 pass (Findings 14/15). Not previously flagged anywhere in this doc's 35 prior Findings. Distinct from `/message/send`, which is deliberately open per Finding 29's explicit confirmation from Gremlin — no equivalent confirmation exists for `/mirror_echo`. **Flagged, not fixed — Gremlin's call**, same posture as every other auth-adjacent finding in this project: gate it the same way its siblings are, or explicitly confirm it's meant to stay open.

  **Correction (2026-07-21), confirmed directly with Gremlin: "MAIN ECHO ENTRY POINT" was never actually true, and this Finding repeated it as fact without checking.** His real primary interface is Echo Studio, run alongside `run.py` in a separate terminal — `/chat/stream`, not `/mirror_echo`. The Pyto phone script is something he runs sometimes, but it was designed as a channel *for Echo's benefit* (ambient real-world context — clipboard, location, sensor data), not as how he talks to her. The comment's own claim was taken at face value the same way this document warns against trusting any other subsystem's self-report — a code comment asserting its own importance is exactly as unverified as `self_model.json` asserting a vector count, and this one was never checked against the one source that could actually confirm or deny it. The underlying security gap is unchanged and still real (an unauthenticated POST endpoint that triggers `dual_learner.log_event()`, `add_to_vector_memory()` writes, and a real `echo_query()` call) — only the "this is Gremlin's main channel" stakes framing was wrong. `run.py`'s comment corrected in the same pass to describe what the route actually is; `CLAUDE.md`'s own "Primary Call Path" architecture section (top of this document) was missing Echo Studio entirely and has been corrected alongside this.
- **Following up on this with Gremlin surfaced the phone symbiote client itself: `thunderhead.py` (tracked in git) and the actual script Gremlin runs on his iPhone (`feral_echo_symbiote.py`, "CLEAN HEADLESS EDITION v2") are the identical file** — same header, same `print("LOADED FROM:", __file__)` line, same code throughout — confirmed by direct comparison, not assumed. The one difference: `thunderhead.py` has `THUNDERHEAD_SECRET` threaded into its `/learning_event`/`/learning_batch` payloads (added in the 2026-07-08 pass per this doc's own Finding 22 Batch 0 note — "`thunderhead.py` updated to send the secret so ... the iPhone symbiote client keep working"); the actual on-phone copy predates that change entirely and sends no secret field at all.
- **The claim in Finding 22 Batch 0 is only half true, and this is the more consequential half of this finding**: even `thunderhead.py`'s "fixed" copy in the repo has `THUNDERHEAD_SECRET = ""` — the plumbing exists, but the real value was never filled in. So copying the current tracked file onto the phone as-is would **still** 403. Practical effect, confirmed by direct read of both endpoints (`run.py:353,376`, `_secret_ok(request.json or {})` on both): every `/learning_event`/`/learning_batch` call the phone client makes gets rejected with 403; `requests.post(...)`'s `if r.ok:` check treats that identically to "Mac unreachable," so the client falls into its own offline-bundling path (`symbiote_bundle.jsonl`) and queues silently forever — real phone telemetry (clipboard, location/activity changes, sensor events) has likely not reached the dual-learner training pipeline at all since the 2026-07-08 auth fix landed, misreported the whole time as ordinary connectivity drops rather than an auth rejection. `/mirror_echo` and `/symbiote_status` are unaffected either way (neither requires a secret server-side), so the conversational mirror path and status telemetry still work.
- **Not fixed — deliberately, and for a reason worth stating plainly**: the correct fix requires pasting the real `GREMLIN_SECRET` value into whichever copy of the script actually runs on the phone. `thunderhead.py` is tracked in git — writing the real secret into *that* file and committing it would leak the same secret every other endpoint's auth depends on straight into git history, exactly the class of mistake Finding 7/R1 already happened once with a different file. Gremlin has the real value in `.env` already; this is flagged for him to do himself when he's ready, not something to fix by pasting a secret into a session's own edits.

**Follow-up (2026-07-18, Finding 42): `/mirror_echo`'s auth gap has a plan in motion, partially rolled out** — see Finding 42 below for the current, more precise version of the phone-script gap described in the three bullets above (the real on-phone script turns out to have no `THUNDERHEAD_SECRET` concept at all, not just an unfilled placeholder).

**Finding 37 — "Tailscale is the boundary" was never actually true; the OS firewall is off and the server is confirmed reachable from a non-Tailscale network (found and fixed 2026-07-16):** Every security finding in this project's history that touches network exposure (R3, R6, Finding 15, Finding 36) rates its severity conditional on "whether Tailscale is really the only thing that can reach port 5000 depends on host firewall config, outside this repo's scope" — flagged and deferred every single time, never independently checked. Checked directly this session: **macOS's Application Firewall was disabled** (`socketfilterfw --getglobalstate` → "Firewall is disabled. (State = 0)"), and `run.py` binds `0.0.0.0:5000` (every interface, confirmed directly, not assumed). At the time of this check the machine had an active tethered connection (a company-issued iPad's hotspot, session-dependent — not Tailscale) at `172.20.10.13`, alongside the real Tailscale interface at `100.84.229.10`. **Verified empirically, not just configured-in-theory**: `curl` against `http://172.20.10.13:5000/admin/liveness-status` returned a real `200`, identical to the same request against the Tailscale IP — meaning any other device on that tethered network had the exact same unauthenticated access as something arriving over Tailscale, for as long as that hotspot session was active. This is the first time in this project's history the "Tailscale is the boundary" assumption was actually tested rather than cited. **Fixed and independently re-verified, same session**: Gremlin enabled the macOS Application Firewall (System Settings → Network → Firewall; confirmed via `socketfilterfw --getglobalstate` → "enabled (State = 1)", not in block-all mode, signed-software auto-allow on). Re-ran the exact same test that found this: the hotspot address (`172.20.10.13`) no longer responds at all (that interface actually dropped its IP entirely in the interim — expected, this connection was a train-tethered hotspot — so this wasn't purely the firewall's doing, but the boundary now holds regardless of network churn). Tailscale access was re-confirmed to still work, 3/3 consecutive requests to `100.84.229.10:5000` after enabling the firewall — one earlier single timeout on that address was a transient network blip (moving train), not the firewall blocking legitimate Tailscale traffic; ruled out by retrying rather than assumed. `localhost` access was also confirmed unaffected throughout. This is now the first network-boundary claim in this project's history that's actually been tested end-to-end rather than cited and deferred.

**Finding 38 — two GitHub repos under this project were public; one had real content exposed for 8 months. Both found, checked, and flipped to private the same session (2026-07-16):** `git remote -v` on this checkout points at `git@github.com:gremlinchode/FeralEcho-Backup.git` — checked directly via `gh api`, not assumed: `visibility: PUBLIC`, and (before this session) genuinely empty (`"Git Repository is empty."`, size 0), so nothing had ever actually been exposed there.
