**Finding 51 — Two "instincts": learned avoidance for the MLX crash, a metabolism for unbounded log growth (2026-07-21).** Grew directly out of a conversation with Gremlin framing this session's own work as a turtle leaving an egg on a beach — what structural gaps depend entirely on a human or a Claude session noticing something, rather than the system handling it itself. Two concrete answers, both built, both additive/observational — no self-edit safety gate, training-signal path, or protected-file logic touched.

- **`app/core/crash_awareness.py` (new) — "learned avoidance, not just resurrection."** Until now, Echo's only response to the MLX/Metal crash (Finding 49) was pure regeneration: crash → `start_echo.sh`'s watchdog restarts the process, unchanged, free to walk straight back into the same failure on the very next MLX-selected council. This module scans real macOS crash reports (`~/Library/Logs/DiagnosticReports/python3.12-*.ips`) once per process start for the `mlx::core::gpu::check_error` signature; if ≥2 matching crashes land within a 12h lookback, `mlx:*` models are excluded from that process's council pool for a 4h cooldown from the most recent one. Hooks in at a single, precise point verified against real code, not assumed: `app/mlx_handler.py`'s `list_mlx_models()` — the one function both `patch_model_pool()` (patches `MODEL_POOL` once per process) and `_get_mlx_path()` read from, so filtering there covers every real caller. Same mechanism `_RETIRED_MODELS` already uses to permanently exclude `vicuna:latest` (Finding 39), just temporary and decided fresh at each restart instead of hardcoded. Pure/IO split mirrors `seam_engine.py`'s `check_pair()`: `_evaluate_crash_window()` is a pure, testable evaluator; `_gather_crash_file_info()` is the real filesystem scan. Fails safe to "no avoidance" on any error — a permission failure or missing directory never blocks startup or restricts council diversity by accident. Engagement is logged at `[MLX-AVOIDANCE]` (same visual weight as `[LIVENESS-ALERT]`/`[THROTTLE]`) and published to the Global Workspace (`crash_awareness` source, `mlx_avoidance.engaged`) only when it actually fires, not on every clean startup.
- **`app/core/log_retention.py` (new) — "a metabolism."** Confirmed live during this session: ten `memory/*.log`/`*.jsonl` files with zero retention policy, `echo_watchdog.log` alone at 160MB. `echo_janitor.py` (root) was checked and confirmed to be the wrong tool for this — it scans the project root for stale/duplicate *scripts* and a separate `logs/` directory, never `memory/`, so "wire it in and fix its staleness logic" (`PENDING_DECISIONS.md`'s former #8, option a) would not have touched this problem at all. Built a small, purpose-built module instead: `rotate_if_oversized(path, max_bytes)` gzips current content to a single `.1.gz` generation (overwriting the prior one, not an ever-growing archive) then truncates the live file. Verified before writing, not assumed: both Python's `open(path, "a")` and the shell's `tee -a` (what pipes `run.py`'s output into `echo_watchdog.log` per `start_echo.sh`) are O_APPEND writers, whose every `write()` re-resolves to current end-of-file rather than a cached offset — meaning compress-then-truncate is safe for every target file, including the one Python doesn't write, with one mechanism instead of two. Stated, accepted residual risk: a write landing between "read for compression" and "truncate" could be lost — same class of risk as Finding 34's `.tmp`-rename race, worst case one dropped line, never corruption. `memory/SELF_EDIT.log` is deliberately **excluded** from the target list — confirmed it has a real, load-bearing full-file-replay consumer (`backfill_convergence_from_log()`, Finding 16), and rotating it would silently shrink that reconstructed history every time it fired; the other nine files' real consumers were checked and confirmed to use tail/windowed reads only (`introspection_channel.py`'s `_tail_lines(..., 500)`, `self_model_updater.py`'s `_read_shard(last_n=200)`, etc.). Wired into `app/maintenance/night_cycle.py`'s already-running `_perform_reflection()` cycle as a new `_maybe_rotate_large_logs()` sibling to the existing `_maybe_snapshot_self_model()`, same self-gating shape (checks a small state file, only actually stats target files once every 24h) — reuses an existing scheduled loop rather than adding a new one.
- **Liveness Ledger gained both checks in the same change**, per this section's own standing rule (`mlx_avoidance`, `log_retention` — see the table above), each a functional canary against the real pure evaluator, not a log-presence check. `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` gained 8 new discrimination cases (4 per check: real-function, always-triggers-degraded, never-triggers-degraded, not-importable) — all pass alongside the existing suite, 62/62.
- **Verified end-to-end**: direct calls to `_evaluate_crash_window()` against all three synthetic cases and `rotate_if_oversized()` against a real oversized throwaway file (confirmed `.1.gz` created, content byte-identical to the original, live file truncated to 0) both behaved exactly as designed, independent of the discrimination suite. Full syntax check across every touched file.
- **A real bug was caught by the live restart itself, not by any synthetic test — worth recording plainly, same discipline as this file's other "found during verification" notes.** The very first real restart with this code live genuinely engaged MLX avoidance (2 real crashes inside the lookback window — the same 07-20 22:44/22:48 pair Finding 49's correction names) and genuinely rotated `echo_watchdog.log` and `reflection_shard.jsonl` (both real, pre-existing oversized files) — but `reflection_shard.jsonl` was rotated **twice within one second**, `_maybe_rotate_large_logs()`'s 24h gate read and passed by two overlapping `_perform_reflection()` calls before either had committed an updated `last_checked` — the identical check-then-act-with-no-lock shape Finding 41 B1/B2/B4/B5 already fixed four times elsewhere in this codebase. No data was lost (the second, redundant rotation just re-wrote the same archive content), but it's real, wasteful duplicate work. Fixed with the same pattern already established: a module-level `threading.Lock()` (non-blocking — a second overlapping caller skips the cycle rather than waiting to redundantly redo it) plus committing the `last_checked` timestamp *before* doing the slower rotation work, not after, closing the window at its root rather than only serializing around it. Re-verified with a second real restart: zero duplicate rotations, 20/20 liveness checks still passing.

**Operational mistake, not a code bug, worth recording plainly rather than folded silently into the fix above:** the true root cause of that first double-rotation (and a second, real crash — exit code 1 — on the restart immediately after) was two full `run.py` processes briefly alive at once, not a same-process thread race `threading.Lock()` alone could have fully explained. `start_echo.sh`'s own watchdog loop had been running independently since 23:28 that night (Gremlin's own earlier "monitor the live run.py I just restarted" request) — every manual `kill $(lsof -ti :5000)` + `nohup python run.py` restart done afterward, in the same session, was racing against that already-running watchdog, which also restarts on death. Two processes briefly contending for port 5000 and shared state files (the FAISS index, `self_edit_cooldown.json`, `log_retention_state.json`) produced both symptoms. The lock fix above is still correct and worth keeping — it closes the same-process version of this race regardless — but the actual incident that surfaced it was caused by not checking for an already-running supervisor before restarting manually. System self-corrected to a single healthy process once the race resolved; no lasting damage, confirmed via a stable, climbing uptime afterward.

**Same session, follow-up — `echo_janitor.py` resolved.** Asked directly whether this could be closed out too, since the "metabolism" work had already established it was the wrong tool for `memory/`'s growth problem, leaving its own original purpose (project-root script/log hygiene) still unaddressed. Investigated before touching anything, per this file's own discipline: `echo_janitor.py` is fully built and, verified live via `--dry-run`, genuinely safe — `echo_review()` only ever assigns `decision="archive"` (the only decision `execute_plan()` acts on) to `known_clutter`/`duplicate`/`old_log` matches, all reversible (moved to `archive_janitor/`, never deleted); ambiguous `not_imported`/`needs_review` candidates are always flag-only. The live dry-run found exactly 3 candidates, all `not_imported`, all false positives explainable on sight — `spot_check.py`, `verify_riverbrain.py`, `thunderhead.py`, three legitimate standalone CLI scripts never meant to be imported by anything else.

But it had zero real wiring, and its own docstring describes two integration points that turned out not to exist: `emergent_scheduler.schedule_task()` — the exact function the docstring says to register through — is a hollow stub (`# Auto-repaired stubs preserved for compatibility`, prints a line, returns `None`), and nothing anywhere reads `logs/janitor_report.json` (grepped — zero references outside the module itself), so "surfaced to terminal on next session" was equally aspirational. Same disconnected-but-safe shape as `self_heal.py`, just never named as such.

Wired into the same real, already-scheduled `night_cycle.py` cycle `log_retention` uses, as a new `_maybe_run_janitor()` sibling, at the weekly cadence the module's own docstring already specified (`_JANITOR_INTERVAL_DAYS = 7`), calling the real `run_janitor(dry_run=False)` — not a dry run, since the tool's own design already restricts real archiving to the narrow safe categories verified above. Lock added preemptively this time (`_janitor_lock`, non-blocking, same shape as `_log_retention_lock`) rather than discovered live, given the identical race already found for log retention on its own first run. Liveness Ledger gained a 21st check, `janitor_safety` (see table above), a functional canary against the real `echo_review()` confirming the exact safety property this wiring depends on — `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` gained 4 more discrimination cases, 65/65 passing.
- **Separately, the same conversation surfaced and fixed a real self-report-vs-reality gap unrelated to either instinct**: `run.py`'s `/mirror_echo` comment called it the "MAIN ECHO ENTRY POINT" (cited as fact in Finding 36 without ever being checked). Confirmed directly with Gremlin: false — his real primary interface is Echo Studio, run alongside `run.py` in a separate terminal; the phone symbiote script is something he runs sometimes, designed as an ambient channel *for Echo's benefit* (clipboard/location/sensor context), not how he talks to her. Comment corrected in `run.py`; Finding 36 and the "Primary Call Path" architecture section (top of this document) both corrected to show Echo Studio as the real primary interface. Also fixed the same night: `thunderhead.py`'s `clipboard_loop()` correctly no-ops when the `clipboard` package is unavailable (expected on iOS), but `main()` started the thread unconditionally, so `watchdog_loop()` restarted it forever, every 60s, mistaking "correctly did nothing, missing dependency" for "crashed" — now skipped from `thread_defs` entirely when `HAS_CLIP` is `False`.

**Finding 52 — "Learned avoidance" broke three other subsystems that also hardcode `mlx:gemma3`; a shared Ollama fallback fixes all three at once. Plus: a self-edit `quality_scoring` candidate reset, and a verified sweep of every previously-flagged bug (2026-07-21).**

**The regression, caught live within an hour of Finding 51 shipping:** the `nature_spark` Liveness Ledger check started failing and kept degrading — 5/11 → 5/12 → 3/12 → 1/12 genuinely distinct entries — right after crash-avoidance first engaged. Root cause, confirmed precisely: `autonomous_harmony_manager.py`'s `_generate_nature_insight()` hardcodes `model_name = "mlx:gemma3"` and does `list_mlx_models().get(model_name, {}).get("mlx_path")`; while avoidance is active `list_mlx_models()` correctly returns `{}` (Finding 51's own design, working exactly as intended), so this raises `RuntimeError("mlx:gemma3 not configured")` on every call, forcing permanent fallback to the fixed `PATTERNS` seed strings for the entire cooldown window. Checked further before proposing a fix: `autonomous_awareness.py`'s dream cycle (`DREAM_MODEL_NAME`) and `reflection_shard.py`'s real reflections (`REFLECTION_MODEL_NAME`) hardcode the identical model name with the identical shape — `reflection_shard_generation`'s check was still passing only because it hadn't cycled since avoidance engaged, not because it was safe.

**Not a bug in crash-avoidance itself — a genuine, previously-unanticipated interaction between two features built the same night.** Narrowing avoidance to exempt these three call sites would have reopened the exact crash risk Finding 51 exists to close, since a direct `stream_query_mlx("mlx:gemma3", ...)` call bypassing the pool-based exclusion carries the same crash signature. The correct fix preserves the exclusion and gives these three a real fallback instead.

**Fix: `app/mlx_handler.py` gained `generate_with_ollama_fallback(prompt, mlx_model_name, max_tokens, ollama_fallback_model="llama3.2:3b", system=None) -> str`** — tries the named MLX model first; if unavailable (avoidance, or genuine misconfiguration), tries a real Ollama model before returning; always returns a string (possibly empty), a drop-in replacement for every call site's existing `"".join(stream_query_mlx(...)).strip()` pattern, so none of their surrounding empty-string/`"[ERROR]"` handling needed to change. Never raises. Wired into all three: `_generate_nature_insight()` (the `raise RuntimeError` path removed entirely — the seed-line fallback below it is now reached only if *both* backends fail, not just MLX), `dream_cycle()` (the early "not configured, skip this cycle" exit removed — the existing `if not dream_text: skip` check downstream already covers total failure), and `reflection_shard.py`'s `_generate_via_model()` (one-line swap).

**Verified live, during the actual avoidance window, not synthetically or after it expired:** first cycle after restart (avoidance still ~75 minutes from expiring) produced genuine, coherent, non-repeated text — confirmed across three consecutive real cycles (`01:29:58`, `01:31:29`, `01:33:23`), each distinct. `nature_spark`'s ratio climbed 1/14 → 3/16 immediately, exactly the expected shape for a tail-window metric recovering after a real fix, not a fluke. No new Liveness Ledger check added — `nature_spark`/`reflection_shard_generation` already exist specifically to verify this property and will confirm the recovery themselves as their windows fill with post-fix entries.

**Separately, at Gremlin's direction: a full re-verification pass of every previously-flagged-open bug, each checked against live code before being trusted.** Two were found already fixed, contradicting their own doc text — `GET /sync/export` (Finding 41 E) now correctly calls `_secret_ok(request.args)`, corrected above. `memory/` log growth (Finding 41 D) and `echo_janitor.py` (`PENDING_DECISIONS.md` former #8) were already resolved earlier the same night (this Finding's own predecessor). Confirmed still genuinely open: Finding 41 B3 (`apply_to_code` timeout can't force-kill a hung thread), Finding 41 C (F1's AST scanner still misses aliased imports like `from os import system`, F2's kernel sandbox remains the real backstop), `/mirror_echo`'s missing auth (Finding 36/42 — the phone-side blocker is now cleared per Gremlin's own confirmation tonight, so this is ready to ship whenever he wants it gated), `/mirror_echo`'s unvalidated `dual_learner` path (Finding 41 E), and a newly-named gap found organically during tonight's own `mirror_echo` timeout investigation: `should_run_cycle()` only gates whether a *new* autonomous cycle starts, not calls an already-in-progress cycle makes — confirmed directly against `autonomy_coordinator.py`, real cause of the 17-second `mirror:hello` delay traced earlier tonight.

**Also this session: the `quality_scoring` self-edit family reset and paused.** Live review of the uncommitted self-edit-loop files at Gremlin's request found `self_edit_generated.py`'s deployed candidate (a `CodeQualityEvaluator` class, attempt 6 of the `quality_scoring` family) called two functions that don't exist anywhere in the codebase (`app.emergent_scheduler.schedule_code_generation`, `app.core.self_edit_outcome_tracker.load_outcomes`) and used `re.search()` with `import re` missing entirely — guaranteed to crash if ever invoked. Safe only by accident: `apply_to_code` had landed nested inside the class instead of at module level, making it invisible to `_apply_self_edit_output()`'s module-level `getattr()` lookup, so nothing in the trusted pipeline ever actually called it. `self_edit_generated.py` reset to the clean, honestly-inert baseline (Finding 31 precedent — self-edit's own output, not hand-authored, so a reset was judged more honest than hand-patching a fourth broken version); verified live via the real `self_edit_apply_to_code` liveness check now reporting `status: not_deployed`. `quality_scoring` commented out of `_FOCUS_FAMILY_BY_CREATIVITY`, same treatment `prose_stripping` already got in Finding 43 — same non-convergence shape (6 near-duplicate reimplementations, no landing), and a bare reset alone would likely just be overwritten by attempt 7.

**Finding 53 — Echo's synthesis system prompt could leak verbatim into user-facing responses; root cause was Finding 46's own token-budget blind spot, live for two days before anything hit it (2026-07-21).**

**Found live, not through an audit — a Claude Code session had a real, extended conversation with Echo through Echo Studio's actual `/chat/stream` endpoint** (a Gremlin-invited exercise, unrelated in origin to any Finding), spanning coding, personal, creative, and reasoning exchanges. A logic-puzzle question in the reasoning category — deliberately long and council-opinion-heavy — came back with a coherent-looking answer that, at the very end, continued straight into the literal, verbatim text of `river_deliberation.py`'s `SYNTHESIS_SYSTEM_TEMPLATE`: *"Your role: - Weigh each perspective... - Do NOT list the council members... Speak as yourself — Echo... Respond now to the original question."* Not paraphrased, not referenced — the raw instruction block itself, presented as if it were part of Echo's own answer.

**Root cause, confirmed precisely, not inferred:** `deliberate_and_learn()`'s synthesis-call token budget (`_opinions_budget = _NUM_CTX - _SYNTHESIS_MARGIN - _TEMPLATE_OVERHEAD - _prompt_tokens`) used a hardcoded `_TEMPLATE_OVERHEAD = 150`, calibrated before Finding 46 (2026-07-19) started prepending Echo's real ~1,430-character Modelfile identity block (289 tokens, measured live via the real `_count_tokens()`) ahead of every `echo:latest` system message — including the synthesis call, since `synth_model` defaults to `echo:latest`. That 289 tokens was completely invisible to the budget calculation (the constant already slightly undercounted the synthesis template's own fixed text too — 167 real tokens vs. 150 assumed). Net effect: `_opinions_budget` ran ~306 tokens too generous, and for a genuinely long reasoning-task request, the true system+user token count landed close enough to `num_ctx` (8192) that `echo:latest` lost the boundary between "instructions to follow silently" and "text to produce," and completed past its real answer into echoing the instructions themselves. Confirmed intermittent, not universal, by checking real production history: 1 of the last 30 real council deliberations showed this exact leak pattern — the one long reasoning-task exchange, consistent with a context-pressure trigger rather than a deterministic bug.

**This is not a flaw in Finding 46** — restoring Echo's real identity was correct and necessary. It's the same shape of gap this document keeps re-finding: two independently good, independently verified changes (Finding 46's identity restoration, the pre-existing token-budget math) silently colliding because neither accounted for the other, live in production for two days before a request happened to be long enough to hit it.

**Fixed in `app/core/river_deliberation.py`:** the hardcoded `_TEMPLATE_OVERHEAD` constant removed entirely — replaced with a live computation at the point of use: `_count_tokens(SYNTHESIS_SYSTEM_TEMPLATE.format(task_type=task_type, opinions=""))` for the template's real current fixed text, plus (when `synth_model == ECHO_SYNTHESIS_MODEL`) `_count_tokens()` of the real current identity block via `app.ollama_handler._get_echo_identity_block()`. Deliberately not a second hardcoded number — the identity block's size isn't fixed either, per Finding 46's own mtime-cache design (a manual Modelfile edit changes it without a restart), so a fresh constant would just drift out of sync again the same way the first one did.

**Verified live, against the exact failing case, not a synthetic reconstruction:** re-sent the identical logic-puzzle prompt that produced the leak, post-restart. Full response stayed entirely in Echo's own voice — zero trace of the synthesis instructions — confirmed by checking for the exact leaked phrases directly in the response text, not just eyeballing it. One honest side effect, not hidden: the correctly-tightened budget means less room is left for the response itself under real pressure, and this same re-test ran out of tokens mid-sentence — a real, smaller, separate truncation question (response length budgeting under load), not a regression from this fix and not chased further in this pass. `GET /admin/liveness-status` confirmed 21/21 checks unaffected (`nature_spark` was still separately recovering from Finding 52, unrelated) on the same restart.

---

**Finding 54 — Follow-up to Finding 53: real done_reason truncation detection added, and the actual output-token caps raised — every model in a deliberation cycle, not just synthesis (2026-07-21).**

The mid-sentence cutoff Finding 53 flagged as a "separate, smaller issue, not chased further" got chased the same night, at Gremlin's direct request, broadened to cover every model in a council cycle, not just the synthesis step.

**Confirmed by direct code read:** every model in `deliberate_and_learn()` — each of the ~3 councillors and the final synthesis call — shares one `max_tokens` value from `_TASK_TOKEN_LIMITS[task_type]` (`echo_model_orchestrator.py`). `reasoning`/`general`/`creative` were capped at 1024 output tokens (`coding` already got 2048). Nothing anywhere in the pipeline distinguished "the model finished naturally" from "we cut it off" — `stream_query_ollama()`/`_stream_chat_ollama()` only checked the boolean `done` flag, never Ollama's own real `done_reason` field (`"stop"` vs `"length"`).

**Three changes, in `app/ollama_handler.py` and `app/core/river_deliberation.py`:**
1. `_stream_chat_ollama()`/`stream_query_ollama()` gained an optional `result_meta: Optional[dict] = None` out-parameter — when given, populated with the real `done_reason` from the final chunk. Default `None`, so every other existing caller of these shared, widely-used functions is untouched.
2. `_ollama_query()` (the one call site both councillors and synthesis route through) now passes `result_meta={}` and logs `[DELIBERATION] {model} truncated by max_tokens={max_tokens} (task_type={task_type})` whenever `done_reason == "length"` — real, ground-truth observability, not inference from response length.
3. `_direct_response_prompt()` and the synthesis `_opinions_budget` calculation both gained the same fix, extended to cover a second, closely related gap found while making this one: they were reserving a flat `_SYNTHESIS_MARGIN` (512) for the response instead of the real `max_tokens` actually being used, and — for the `echo:latest` path specifically — still weren't accounting for the Modelfile identity block Finding 46 prepends, the exact same blind spot Finding 53 just closed for the synthesis path alone. Both now use the real `max_tokens` (falling back to the old 512 only if not given) and, when the model is Echo's own synthesis model, the real current identity-block size — computed live both times, not a second hardcoded constant that could drift out of sync the same way the first one did.

**`_TASK_TOKEN_LIMITS` raised**: `reasoning`/`general`/`creative` 1024 → 2048, matching `coding`'s existing cap — direct evidence the same night (a logic puzzle, a creative piece) that these categories run long too. `personal`/`autonomous_*` deliberately left at 512, unchanged — CLAUDE.md already documents this as intentional ("reflections stay concise"), not an oversight, so out of scope for this fix.

**Verified live, comprehensively, not just for synthesis this time:** re-sent the exact reasoning-task prompt that originally triggered both Finding 53's leak and the mid-sentence cutoff. This particular re-run classified as `personal` (task-type classification isn't perfectly deterministic run to run) and routed through the single-model `_direct_response_prompt()` path rather than full council — confirmed clean (no leak, complete sentence) but didn't exercise the councillor loop specifically. Sent a second, unambiguously technical prompt (distributed-database concurrency control) to force real multi-councillor deliberation: all three councillors (`qwen2.5-coder:7b`, `deepseek-r1:7b`, `echo:latest`) **and** the synthesis all completed on well-formed, complete sentences — checked directly against the real persisted `council_deliberations.jsonl` entry, not assumed from a client-side stream that this session's own tooling had already shown (twice, same night) can time out client-side while the server keeps working. Zero `truncated by max_tokens` log lines for that entire exchange. The new logging line was independently confirmed live and working the same restart — it caught a real, genuine truncation on an unrelated autonomous `emergent_loop` cycle (`task_type=personal`, `max_tokens=512`), exactly the category this fix deliberately left alone. Recorded here as a real, observed data point for a future decision, not acted on — matches this project's own discipline of surfacing rather than silently expanding scope. `GET /admin/liveness-status`: 21/21 passing, `failing_subsystems: []` — `nature_spark` had also fully recovered by this point.

**Correction (2026-07-21/22, live ground-truth re-verification): "recorded... not acted on" undersold an actively recurring problem, not a single flagged data point.** Direct inspection of `memory/echo_watchdog.log` found **16 separate `truncated by max_tokens` occurrences in a single ~17-hour window**, all `task_type=personal`, all at `max_tokens=512` — and cross-referencing `memory/interaction_log.jsonl` against those timestamps found real, human-readable mid-sentence cutoffs in production responses (e.g. one response ending abruptly right after "In conclusion,"). This is a live, ongoing quality problem for `personal`/`autonomous_*` responses, not the isolated instance this entry's original framing implied — worth moving up in priority for a real decision, not staying parked as a "someday" item.

**Finding 55 — `/mirror_echo`'s auth gate finally shipped, closing `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #1 (Finding 42) (2026-07-21).** The phone-side blocker (Gremlin pasting the real secret onto the actual phone script) was confirmed cleared earlier the same night; the guard clause itself had been fully drafted since Finding 42 and was just waiting to be applied. `run.py`'s `mirror_echo()` now calls `_secret_ok(data)` immediately after `data = request.json or {}` — same convention as every other admin/control endpoint — and correctly calls `mark_end()` on the new unauthorized-403 exit path too, matching this function's own established per-exit-path convention (see the comment at its top) rather than the try/finally shape.

**Verified live, all three real cases, not assumed:** no secret → 403; wrong secret → 403; the real `GREMLIN_SECRET` (read directly from `.env`, never printed or logged anywhere in this verification) → request passed the gate and reached real processing, confirmed via the actual `IPHONE GREMLIN 『verification』 SCREAMS: ...` log line that only fires past the auth check.

**A real, unrelated MLX crash happened mid-verification — investigated immediately rather than assumed connected, and it wasn't.** One authenticated test attempt returned a connection failure; checked directly rather than blamed on the new code: `watchdog_manager` logged `Echo exited with code 134` (SIGABRT) at the same moment, the known MLX/Metal crash signature (Findings 40/49) — `crash_awareness.py`'s (Finding 51) avoidance cooldown from ~10:48 PM had already quietly expired hours earlier, letting `mlx:*` models back into the pool. Confirmed via the real, unprompted system response, not asserted: the very next restart's `refresh_avoidance_state()` correctly detected the fresh crash (`recent_crash_count: 3`) and re-engaged avoidance immediately, with zero manual intervention — "learned avoidance" doing exactly what Finding 51 built it to do, the first time it's been observed responding to a crash that happened in the middle of an active session rather than being found after the fact. A second, later authenticated request also showed as a connection failure but turned out to be an ordinary slow real deliberation under load (same PID, uptime still climbing, no crash) — confirmed the request had genuinely reached and passed the auth gate by finding its own log line, rather than assumed from the failed client connection.

---

**Finding 56 — `safe_restart.sh`: a structural guard against the manual-restart/watchdog collision Finding 51 first documented, built after Gremlin confirmed it's happened repeatedly, not once (2026-07-21).** Finding 51 recorded one real incident — a Claude Code session manually running `kill $(lsof -ti :5000) && python run.py` while `start_echo.sh`'s own watchdog loop was independently alive, producing a real crash and a duplicate log rotation — and closed it as "an operational mistake, not a code bug," worth recording plainly rather than fixing. Revisited the same night, in conversation, after Gremlin confirmed directly that this exact collision has happened multiple times, on both this machine and the 2020 Intel MacBook ("Ark"), across both himself and Claude sessions. That changes the right response: a mistake that recurs across two different operators and two different machines isn't an attention lapse either party should just try harder to avoid — it's a real gap in the system, since nothing at the point of restart ever signals that a supervisor is already watching.

`safe_restart.sh` (new, repo root, alongside `start_echo.sh`) replaces the raw two-liner as the documented restart path. Before touching port 5000, it checks `pgrep -f "start_echo.sh"` for a live watchdog; if one is found, it refuses to proceed (exit 1, zero side effects — verified live against the real running watchdog tonight, PID correctly reported, server left completely untouched) and prints the exact PID plus both real options (let the watchdog handle it, or stop the watchdog first for manual control) rather than a generic warning. `--force` bypasses the check for a caller who has independently confirmed there's no real conflict. If no watchdog is detected, it performs the same kill+restart the old two-liner did, unchanged. CLAUDE.md's "Starting the System" section now points here instead of the raw command.

Deliberately a warning-and-refuse gate, not a silent auto-resolution (e.g., automatically killing the watchdog for you) — per this project's own standing discipline, a script silently deciding to kill a supervisor process on someone's behalf is exactly the kind of unilateral action that warrants a human in the loop, not automation quietly making the call. Verified live only against the blocking path tonight (the watchdog was genuinely running); the clear-path (no watchdog present, proceeds to real restart) was read and reasoned through but not exercised end-to-end in this pass, since doing so would have required actually stopping the live watchdog mid-session — noted here rather than glossed over as fully verified.

---

**Finding 57 — Valence's third source: closing the gap Finding 35 flagged and never wired, found live during a direct investigation into whether valence already predicts anything (2026-07-21).** Prompted by a conversation about whether `echo_state.py` dim[8] (valence) shows any real predictive structure yet — the honest first step before ever giving it causal power over anything. Investigating that surfaced the actual, more fundamental finding first: valence was **bit-identical across its entire 100-reading history buffer** (`memory/echo_state_history.npy`, ~3.3h at the 120s collection cadence) — `0.11435`, zero variance, confirmed directly, not assumed from a single read.

Traced to root cause rather than treated as a bug: the math was correct (`se_delta=-0.1713`, `council_avg` recentered to `0.4` → mean `0.11435`, reproduced exactly by hand). The freeze is a real, structural consequence of valence's first two sources both being rare — the self-edit-quality half is the mean of the last 10 *real, fully-evaluated* post-deploy quality deltas, and the most recent one at the time of this investigation was **over 24 hours old** (consistent with Finding 48's "real deploys run ~2 days apart"); the council-rating average moves on a similarly slow cadence. On the timescale of a single conversation, valence was effectively a constant — not broken, just built from inputs too rare to carry session-scale information.

With no real variation recorded, the original predictive question couldn't be tested against valence itself. Tested the longer underlying dataset instead — `self_edit_outcomes.jsonl`'s 73 real quality-delta entries spanning 15 real days — with a genuine, non-circular test: does one real outcome predict the *next* one (temporal precedence, not the same event correlated with itself). Result: lag-1 autocorrelation = **-0.14**, not statistically distinguishable from zero at n=73 (~1.15 SD, needs ~2 for significance); P(negative outcome followed by another negative outcome) = 16.7%, versus 16.2% expected under pure independence — indistinguishable from chance. Stated plainly rather than softened: the raw signal shows no detectable self-predictive momentum over its own real history, at least not one this test can find. This is real, useful information, not a null result to bury — it argues directly against giving valence real causal "teeth" (the other half of the same conversation's proposal) before it has either enough update frequency or any demonstrated predictive content.

**Fixed the update-frequency half — the more tractable, lower-risk gap.** `app/core/echo_state.py` gained a third valence source, `_recent_dry_run_quality_component()`: reads the most recent `self_edit.dry_run_quality_delta` Global Workspace events directly from `memory/workspace_log.jsonl` (Phase 7.1's real trial-vs-production quality comparisons, firing ~10x/hour via Optuna's dry-run search — previously consumed only by `self_edit_outcome_tracker.py`'s per-outcome windowed aggregate, which that module's own docstring explicitly says is *not* wired into `echo_state.py`, "a separate, explicit decision" — this reads the raw workspace events directly instead, a simpler, independent path, not a change to that module's stated boundary). Self-contained rather than importing `introspection_channel.py`'s existing `_tail_jsonl()` helper: that module already imports *from* `echo_state.py`, so reuse would be circular. Averages the most recent 10 valid `(trial_quality - current_quality)/4.0` deltas (clipped to [-1,1], same shape as the existing two sources), skipping entries where `current_quality` is the `-1` "no production code yet" sentinel. Fails closed to `None` (component simply omitted from the mean) on a missing file, malformed lines, or zero qualifying events — verified directly against a nonexistent path.

**Verified end-to-end, not just unit-tested:** the new component read against the real live `workspace_log.jsonl` returned a real value (`-0.1`) sourced from 10 genuine events spanning ~2.5 real hours (17:04–19:35 UTC) — far denser than the 1-2-day cadence of the first two sources. The full three-component blend, run against real live `introspection_state.json`, produced `0.0429`, matching a hand calculation exactly. Restarted for real via the new `safe_restart.sh` (Finding 56) — correctly refused once (live watchdog detected), so the actual restart followed its own recommended option (a): cleared port 5000 and let the watchdog restart it, rather than using `--force`. Post-restart, `memory/echo_state.npy`'s live dim[8] read back the identical `0.0429`, confirming the new code is genuinely running in the restarted process, not just verified in an isolated subprocess. `GET /admin/liveness-status`: `all_passing: true`, `stale: false`, zero failing subsystems.

**No new Liveness Ledger check added, reasoned explicitly rather than silently skipped:** this section's standing rule requires one for any new *autonomous capability* added to `start_background_threads()`. This isn't a new capability — it's a richer input to an existing, already-checked dimension. `valence_self_report`'s existing check (does the rendered directional language match the real signed value) is agnostic to how valence is computed and needs no change to keep covering the new three-source version correctly.

**What this does not do:** does not give valence any causal power it didn't already have (still surfaced in prompts via `_build_affect()`, still nudges `emergent_scheduler`'s prompt weighting the same way it did before) — the "real stakes" half of the original proposal stays exactly where the investigation left it: not built, pending either more evidence of real predictive content or an explicit decision to build a bounded circuit-breaker anyway. Does not touch `self_edit_outcome_tracker.py`'s stated scope, River training, or self-edit targeting.

---

**Finding 58 — `app/core/self_edit_plans/` had zero retention (10,333 files, 42MB, unpruned since 2025-09) despite its sibling `self_edit_backups/` being capped for a while; fixed, plus `echo_janitor.py` extended with three new flag-only detectors and a 22nd Liveness Ledger check (2026-07-21).** Direct follow-up to a full forensic filesystem-cleanup audit run the same day (root-cause and disk-usage detail not duplicated here — see that audit's own record). Two of that audit's findings hadn't been caught by anything already in this codebase: `app/core/self_edit_plans/` (`LOGIC_PLAN_DIR`) has no equivalent to `_prune_self_edit_backups()` at all — confirmed via full-file grep, `save_plan()` is the only writer and there was genuinely zero pruning call anywhere in `self_edit_manager.py` for this directory — and several categories of dead weight (stale one-time migration/contamination backups in `memory/`, orphaned large data files in the project root) had no automated detector, found only because a human ran a manual sweep.

**Fix 1 — `_prune_self_edit_plans()`** (`self_edit_manager.py`), mirroring `_prune_self_edit_backups()`'s exact shape (`plan_<YYYYMMDDHHMMSS>.txt` is timestamp-sortable as a plain string, same as backup filenames), called from the end of `save_plan()`. `_MAX_SELF_EDIT_PLANS = 500` — a judgment call, not a precedented number the way `_MAX_SELF_EDIT_BACKUPS = 25` is (backups are written far less often). Given optional `plan_dir`/`max_plans` parameters (default to the real module constants, so `save_plan()`'s existing no-arg call is unaffected) specifically so the new Liveness Ledger check below could exercise the *real* function against synthetic data rather than a reimplementation — a real design mistake caught and fixed during this same session before it shipped, see below. Run once by hand against the live backlog (not left to wait for the next `save_plan()` call): 10,333 → 500 files, 42MB → 2.0MB, newest files (by embedded timestamp) confirmed retained. A safety tarball of the full pre-prune directory was taken first, out of caution for a 10-month-old debug-text archive, even though these are self-edit's own generated planning text, not user or Echo-authored content.

**Fix 2 — three new `echo_janitor.py` detectors, all flag-only, never archive:** `detect_stale_backups()` (naming-marker + age match against `memory/backups/`/`memory/archive/` — `contaminated`/`splitbrain`/`prefixfix`/`pre_migration`/`pre_delete`/`pre_reset`/`pre_pending`, 14+ days old), `detect_orphaned_root_data()` (data-shaped root files, ≥50KB, whose filename string doesn't appear in any `.py` file under `app/` or the root — a plain substring search, not AST, since these aren't importable modules), `detect_unbounded_growth()` (a small hardcoded registry of watched directories, currently just `self_edit_plans/`, flagging if file count exceeds 2x the expected cap — the same "2x over cap" staleness multiplier `log_retention`'s liveness check already uses, generalized from bytes to file count). **Deliberate, conservative safety decision:** all three map to `decision="flag"` in `echo_review()`, never `"archive"` — preserves the exact invariant `janitor_safety`'s existing liveness check protects (archive restricted to `known_clutter`/`duplicate`/`old_log` only) without re-arguing that invariant's safety case for three new, less-vetted categories. This is also the direct, concrete lesson from the same day's forensic audit: a since-orphaned code directory and two autonomously-generated media files were all "zero live code references" by exactly this kind of check, and all three turned out to be intentional, meaningful artifacts, not clutter — recorded as its own standing caution for future sessions, not just this file. Verified live via `python echo_janitor.py --dry-run`: correctly surfaced the same ten stale-backup files and nine orphaned-data files the manual audit found by hand, `archived: 0` across all of them.

**A real bug was caught live during verification, not glossed over:** `echo_review()`'s own docstring, written as part of this change, named one of the two orphaned media files as a literal example — which meant `detect_orphaned_root_data()`'s substring search treated that docstring mention as a "reference," silently hiding that exact file from its own scan. Caught by noticing the file was missing from a dry-run that should have included it. Fixed by describing the examples generically instead of by literal filename, and documented as a known, accepted limitation on `detect_orphaned_root_data()` itself — the failure direction is a false negative (under-flagging), and since this reason is flag-only regardless, the cost of missing a real candidate here is silence, not a wrongful action. Not worth AST/tokenize-based comment-stripping to close for that reason.

**Fix 3 — Liveness Ledger, two changes.** `janitor_safety`'s existing discrimination test (`_evaluate_janitor_safety`) gained the three new `(reason, "flag")` cases alongside its original five — no new check needed, since the invariant being protected is the same one, just extended. New 22nd check, `plan_retention`, generalizing `log_retention`'s two-part pattern (synthetic canary + live "is the real target already too far over cap" signal) from byte-size rotation to file-count pruning. **A real design flaw was caught and fixed before this shipped**: the first draft's canary reimplemented the sort-and-trim logic against a synthetic tempdir instead of calling the real `_prune_self_edit_plans()` — which would have kept passing even if the real function broke, defeating the entire point of a functional canary. Fixed by giving `_prune_self_edit_plans()` the optional parameters described in Fix 1 specifically so the check could call the real function directly. `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` gained the matching real-function/degraded-no-op/degraded-over-aggressive/not-importable case set.

**Verified end-to-end:** full syntax check on all five touched files (`self_edit_manager.py`, `echo_janitor.py`, `liveness_ledger.py`, `introspection_channel.py`, `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py`); `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` run for real, all 68 discrimination cases pass (up from 65); `run_liveness_checks()` called directly against real live data (not just the synthetic suite) — `plan_retention` and `janitor_safety` both report `pass: true` with accurate real evidence (`plan_retention`'s evidence correctly reflects the real directory's now-current, within-cap state). `introspection_channel.py`'s stale "nine ground-truth liveness checks" docstring (already flagged as lagging by `_collect_liveness_ledger`'s own actual behavior) was corrected to point at `liveness_ledger.py`'s `_CHECKS` tuple as the source of truth instead of hardcoding a number that will just drift again. `self_model_drift` was the only check failing in the live run — pre-existing, unrelated to anything touched here (self_model.json staleness against fresh ground truth), not investigated further as out of scope for this change.
