**Finding 59 — Two small findings from a live-restart observation sweep (2026-07-21): a real, persistent Reddit fetch credential failure, and a log-retention gap in the same class as Finding 58's — the second one fixed, the first flagged (needs a real credential, not something fixable in code).** Prompted directly by watching a real restart's log output rather than a targeted investigation.

**Reddit fetch (`app/internet_tools/`, exact module not further traced this pass) fails on every run, not intermittently.** `Reddit Science`/`Reddit Philosophy`/`Reddit WorldNews` each log `"Unauthorized or forbidden (check API key). Disabling for the rest of this run."` — confirmed to recur identically across two consecutive fresh restarts (15:12 and 15:23 the same day), ruling out a transient blip. 3 of the ~13 documented internet-fetch sources are silently down every run as a result. **Not fixed — no code bug found, this needs a real, working Reddit API credential in `.env`, which isn't something fixable from inside a session.** Flagged rather than defaulted into a code change that wouldn't address the actual cause.

**`memory/scripture_warnings.log` (976KB, 2263 entries spanning 2026-06-27–present at time of finding) was never added to `night_cycle.py`'s `_LOG_RETENTION_TARGETS`**, the same nine-file list Finding 51 built — a tenth, smaller, slower-growing file (~40KB/day observed) that simply wasn't included the first time. **Fixed**: added with a 10MB cap (smaller than its 50-100MB siblings, matched to its real observed growth rate rather than copied from the others). Verified: file is currently at 9.2% of its new cap, syntax-clean, full liveness discrimination suite unaffected (still 68/68 — `log_retention`'s check reads `_LOG_RETENTION_TARGETS` live and correctly saw the new entry with no stale-file alarm, since it's nowhere near 2x over cap).

**A third, unrelated clarity fix caught in the same sweep**: `system_guard.py`'s throttle log line printed all three raw readings unlabeled (`"RAM=84.1% free=781.60GB load=1.9"`) with no indication of which metric actually crossed a threshold — `free_gb` is disk free space for `memory/` (`memory_dir_free_gb`), not RAM, but sitting directly next to a RAM percentage it reads like 781GB of free RAM. The throttle *decision* itself was always correct; only the log's own clarity was the problem, and a reader had to know the threshold constants by heart to attribute cause correctly. Fixed to explicitly name which metric(s) crossed their threshold (with the threshold value inline) ahead of the full raw readings, and relabeled `free` to `disk_free`. Verified directly against today's exact real values (`RAM=84.1%, free=781.60GB, load=1.9`): now correctly attributes the trigger to `RAM=84.1% (>80%)` alone, leaving the other two out of the trigger list since neither crossed its own threshold.

---

**Finding 60 — Idea researched and designed, not built: council review of `echo_janitor.py`'s flag-only candidates, mirroring the Dissent Log's already-proven pattern (2026-07-21).** Gremlin floated the idea directly: could FeralEcho's council deliberate on whether to keep or remove files `echo_janitor.py` flags but doesn't auto-archive? Researched against the one mechanism in this codebase that already proves multi-model file/code review can work reliably here — `self_edit_manager.py`'s `_council_review_core_edit()`/`_build_dissent_entry()`/`_log_dissent_entry()` (Finding 9, the Dissent Log) — rather than designed from scratch.

**Why the Dissent Log's shape, specifically, and not a more direct approach:** `echo_janitor.py`'s own `echo_review()` docstring already tried and explicitly rejected the more direct version of this idea — "Echo's identity is too strongly embedded to reliably output raw JSON, so structured file decisions are handled by explicit rules here." The Dissent Log sidesteps exactly that failure mode: it never asks a model for structured JSON, only a constrained one-line verdict (`APPROVE`/`REJECT`) plus one sentence of rationale — a format proven reliable in production for protected-file edit proposals since Finding 9.

**Sketched design, following that template closely, not attempted as a novel one:**
- `_council_review_janitor_candidate(candidate: dict) -> dict` — same `rank_models(task_type=...)[:3]` + per-model `query_ollama()` call shape as `_council_review_core_edit()`, but requesting a three-way verdict instead of two (`SAFE_TO_ARCHIVE` / `LIKELY_MEANINGFUL_KEEP` / `UNCERTAIN`), since "keep or remove" isn't the same symmetric question as "approve or reject a diff." Only applies to the categories that are already flag-only today (`not_imported`, `needs_review`, `stale_backup`, `orphaned_data`, `unbounded_growth`) — never the three tightly-scoped, already-archive-eligible categories, which don't need a second opinion on a decision already made safely.
- **Structurally incapable of ever producing an `"archive"` decision, not just policy-restricted from it.** The council's opinion would be logged as an additional `council_opinion` field on the candidate dict and to a new `memory/janitor_council_log.jsonl` (same shape as `dissent_log.jsonl` — logs every review including unanimous `LIKELY_MEANINGFUL_KEEP` ones, for honest auditability, same discipline as `_log_dissent_entry()`), surfaced in `logs/janitor_report.json` alongside the existing `decision`/`echo_reason` fields. `execute_plan()` itself would be completely untouched — it would keep reading only `decision`, which `echo_review()` would keep setting exactly as it does today, regardless of what the council said. This mirrors the Dissent Log precedent exactly: `propose_core_edit()` never reads its own council's verdict to decide whether to write anything either.
- Would need its own Liveness Ledger check (per this section's own standing rule for new autonomous capabilities) verifying this invariant specifically: that `_council_review_janitor_candidate()`'s output can never influence `echo_review()`'s `decision` field, mirroring `janitor_safety`'s existing discrimination shape exactly — not a check on whether the council's *opinions* are good, a check on whether they're structurally incapable of mattering to the one decision that actually moves files.

**The real, named risk — the reason this is flagged rather than built today:** council review has zero access to the kind of context that already fooled a *more conservative* heuristic check twice this same session — `RebelCode/`, `Figure_*.png`, and `WhisperOfPeace.wav` all read as "zero live code references, safe to flag" to `detect_orphaned_root_data()`, and all three turned out to be intentional, meaningful artifacts a plain grep couldn't know about. A council reasoning from a filename, a size, and (for text files only) a content excerpt has strictly *less* information than that grep did, not more — for binary media files specifically (images, audio), there's no content preview possible at all, so the council would be working from even thinner surface features than the heuristic that already got it wrong twice. This isn't a reason the idea is bad — the design above never lets a council opinion cause an action regardless of how confident it sounds — but it is the reason the opinion has to be presented to a human reviewer as one more (fallible) input, not as anything resembling authority, and it's why this project's own established discipline (report, design, pause, let Gremlin decide) applies here rather than defaulting into a build.

**Not built. Flagged for Gremlin's call** — see `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #12.

---

**Finding 61 — Two of the ~13 autonomous fetch sources (Finding 6/CLAUDE.md's own "Autonomous Background Path" section) had been silently storing garbage instead of real content since they were added; fixed (2026-07-21).** Gremlin asked directly for a quality/coherence inspection of the fetched news sources. Sampled real, live stored entries from `memory/memory_meta.json` (`meta.role == "fetch"`) rather than reasoning about the fetch code in the abstract.

**StackOverflow**: every stored entry was a raw Python dict repr of the entire API response (`"[StackOverflow] {'items': [{'tags': ['python'], 'owner': {'account_id': 46757828, ...'`) — irrelevant internals like owner profile-image URLs, not the actual question. **Wikipedia AI**: same class of bug — entries stored a raw dict repr with embedded HTML markup (`<span class="mw-page-title-main">...`) instead of real text. Root cause, identical for both: `app/internet_tools/autonomous_fetch.py`'s `fetch_and_log()` JSON-shape dispatch only special-cased a few known shapes (NewsAPI's `articles`, Reddit's `data.children`, NASA's `explanation`, muffinlabs' `data.Events`, plain lists) — StackOverflow's real shape (`{"items": [...], "has_more": ..., "quota_max": ...}`, each item carrying a genuine `title` field) and Wikipedia's real shape (a flat dict with a genuinely well-written `extract` field) were never added, so both fell through to the generic `str(data)[:500]` dump. Confirmed live against the real APIs before writing the fix, not assumed from documentation.

**Fixed**: two new shape cases added to `fetch_and_log()`, following the existing branch pattern exactly (`isinstance(data.get("items"), list)` for StackOverflow — extracts `title`/`score`/`is_answered` per item; `"extract" in data` for Wikipedia — extracts `title`/`extract`). Verified directly against the real live APIs through the actual production call path (`fetch_and_log()` itself, not a reimplementation): StackOverflow now stores `"[StackOverflow] Taking File Data (score -5, unanswered)"` and Wikipedia now stores `"[Wikipedia AI] Artificial intelligence: Artificial intelligence (AI) is the capability of computational systems to perform tasks typically associated with human intelligence..."` — real, coherent, on-topic content instead of raw dumps. Full liveness discrimination suite unaffected (68/68 — no check reads this file).

**Confirmed genuinely good, same sampling pass**: The Guardian, Hacker News, arXiv (AI/Biology/Cosmology), This Day History — all coherent, well-formed, properly extracted. **Reddit — still broken**, consistent with the same-day finding above (Finding 59): all 3 subreddits fail `"Unauthorized or forbidden"` every run; needs a real working credential, not a code fix.

**Two secondary observations, flagged but not chased to a root cause in this pass:** (1) the exact same "This Day History" snippet was found stored **53 times** in `memory_meta.json`, including pairs only 6–11 seconds apart — the FAISS-cosine dedup check (`_is_duplicate()`, >0.92 similarity threshold) should catch an exact string match and evidently isn't reliably. (2) Fetch cycles were observed roughly 8–11 minutes apart during this session, well under `autonomous_loop.py`'s documented `AUTONOMOUS_SLEEP = 3600` (bounded to 1800–5400s by `_compute_next_sleep()`) — the timing correlates closely with two server restarts during the same conversation (each restart likely fires an immediate first cycle before interval-based pacing takes over), but this wasn't fully verified as the complete explanation. Neither investigated further as out of scope for the parsing fix itself.

---

**Finding 62 — Correction to Finding 61's two "secondary observations": both fully explained by a real, confirmed duplication between two independent autonomous loops, not restart timing; fixed the same day (2026-07-21).** Observing a real restart (the fix-verification pass for Finding 61) turned up the actual cause: `app/autonomous_loop.py`'s own `ThreadPoolExecutor` fetch pool and `app/core/autonomous_loop_with_optuna.py`'s `autonomous_loop_iteration()` (driving `ModelGuidedOrchestrator`, per Finding 28) each ran their **own independent, full fetch pass** against every `FETCH_SOURCES` entry — confirmed directly in `autonomous_loop_iteration()`'s own docstring ("1. Fetch content from all registered sources") and by tracing StackOverflow-specific log lines through a full day: the two loops' fetch attempts landed 5–10 seconds apart, dozens of times (00:49, 01:12, 02:16, 06:46, 09:15, 13:46, 15:12, 15:31, 15:55...). Finding 28 already knew this second loop does its own fetch step, but never connected it to duplicating the first loop's work — the same "two independent uncoordinated timers on one shared resource" shape Finding 28 already fixed once for this exact pair of loops' Optuna calls (`try_run_optuna()`'s shared atomic gate), never extended to the fetch step both loops also happen to run.

**Fixed**, mirroring `try_run_optuna()`'s own pattern exactly: `app/internet_tools/autonomous_fetch.py` gained `claim_fetch_cycle()` — an atomic, lock-guarded check-and-claim (`threading.Lock` + a module-level timestamp, in-memory only, same posture as `_DISABLED_SOURCES`) gating a shared 1800s cooldown (half of `autonomous_loop.py`'s own base 3600s interval). Both loops now call it right before their existing fetch section; whichever loop's turn comes up first claims the cycle and proceeds exactly as before, the other logs a skip and moves straight to the rest of its own cycle (sandbox, Optuna, tool discovery) unaffected — neither loop's non-fetch behavior was touched. Verified directly: 20 concurrent threads calling `claim_fetch_cycle()` simultaneously produced exactly 1 winner, confirming the lock holds under real race conditions, not just sequential calls. Full liveness discrimination suite unaffected (no check references this module).

---

**Finding 63 — Finding 60 built: council review of `echo_janitor.py`'s flag-only candidates, exactly as designed and flagged for Gremlin's call, now implemented (2026-07-21).** Gremlin approved the design from Finding 60 directly. Built to the sketch, not a redesign: `_council_review_janitor_candidate()` (`echo_janitor.py`) mirrors `_council_review_core_edit()`'s proven shape — `rank_models(task_type="reasoning")[:3]` + a constrained one-line verdict (`SAFE_TO_ARCHIVE` / `LIKELY_MEANINGFUL_KEEP` / `UNCERTAIN`, explicitly told to say `UNCERTAIN` rather than guess) plus one sentence of rationale per model, never raw JSON. A bounded, text-only content preview (`_read_content_preview()`, 2000 chars, extension-gated) is included when available; binary/media candidates get none, honestly reflecting the thinner signal Finding 60 named as the real risk.

**Structurally, not just procedurally, incapable of ever archiving anything.** `_attach_council_opinions()` — the integration point wired into `run_janitor()` right after `echo_review()` — only ever *reads* `candidate["decision"]` (to select which flag-only candidates to review) and *reads* `"path"`/`"reason"`/`"detail"`; it never assigns to `"decision"` anywhere in the function. The council's verdict is attached as a separate `council_opinion` field only. Every review (including confident ones) is logged to `memory/janitor_council_log.jsonl`, mirroring the Dissent Log's own "log the empty/negative case too" discipline, and a `_recently_reviewed()` staleness gate (7-day window, matching the weekly cadence) skips re-querying models for a file that's still flagged but was already reviewed this week. `run_janitor()` gained `run_council_review: bool = True` (default on for the real weekly autonomous cycle; a new `--no-council` CLI flag opts out for fast manual testing).

**New Liveness Ledger check, `janitor_council_advisory_only`** (23rd) — not a check on whether the council's opinions are *good*, a check on whether they're structurally incapable of mattering to the one decision that actually moves files, mirroring `janitor_safety`'s own invariant-protection shape. Feeds `_attach_council_opinions()` a synthetic council that returns the most confident-sounding possible verdict (`SAFE_TO_ARCHIVE` from all three fake models) and confirms `decision` still reads `"flag"` afterward.

**A real bug was caught and fixed while building this check, not after:** the check's fixed synthetic candidate path got a real entry written to the real, persistent `janitor_council_log.jsonl` on its first run — meaning every subsequent run (this check fires every 120s via `introspection_channel`) saw that path as "recently reviewed" via the real staleness gate and silently skipped review, making the check's own evidence wrong (`"council_opinion field wasn't attached... at all"`) on the second and every later call. Fixed by redirecting `echo_janitor.JANITOR_COUNCIL_LOG_PATH` to a fresh temp file for the duration of the canary call only, restored immediately after — verified idempotent by running the full suite twice in a row with identical, correct results.

**Verified end-to-end, with a real LLM call, not just synthetic cases:** `_council_review_janitor_candidate()` run directly against `imports.txt` (a real flagged candidate) produced three genuinely distinct, appropriately-hedged opinions — `echo:latest` and `deepseek-r1:7b` both said `UNCERTAIN`, explicitly citing insufficient context; `gemma3:4b` said `LIKELY_MEANINGFUL_KEEP`, reasoning about the file's likely role — zero false `SAFE_TO_ARCHIVE` confidence on a genuinely ambiguous file, exactly the honestly-hedged behavior the design was hoping for. Full `python echo_janitor.py --dry-run` re-run afterward: `archived: 0` held across all 23 flagged candidates, matching the invariant this whole feature depends on. Discrimination suite: 70 cases (up from 68), all passing.

**`PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #12 closed** — this was its only open question (whether/how to build this), now answered by building exactly the design that item pointed to.

---

**Finding 64 — `crash_awareness.py` was trusting unreliable file `mtime` instead of the real crash timestamp, causing false/extended MLX avoidance triggers; fixed the same day it was found, but never given its own Finding until this retroactive pass (2026-07-21).** Found live while observing a real restart at Gremlin's request: two real crash reports (dated 2026-07-15/16 by their own filenames and embedded JSON headers) had their file `mtime` silently updated to the current day, within seconds of real avoidance-check timestamps in `memory/echo_watchdog.log` — making a 5-6-day-old crash look artificially fresh. This explained the morning's `"3 MLX-signature crash(es)... most recent ~0.0h ago"` avoidance trigger, which was actually reacting to a stale report, not a new crash.

**Root cause confirmed, not assumed:** `_gather_crash_file_info()` used `os.path.getmtime(path)` as its only signal for "when did this crash happen." A plain Python read was directly tested against a copy of one of the affected files and confirmed **not** to change its `mtime`, ruling out this module's own file access as the cause — most likely macOS's own background crash-report processing (symbolication, Spotlight indexing, or similar) touches these files well after the real crash.

**Fixed:** every `.ips` crash report's own JSON header (its first line) carries an authoritative `timestamp` field (e.g. `"2026-07-16 20:20:41.00 -0700"`) that isn't subject to this drift. `_gather_crash_file_info()` now parses that field via a new `_parse_ips_header_timestamp()`, falling back to `mtime` (logged as a fallback, not silently trusted as equally good) only if the header is ever unparseable.

**Verified directly, not assumed:** re-parsed the two real affected files after the fix — both now correctly report their true, several-days-old crash time instead of the false "today." Re-ran `_evaluate_crash_window()` against the real, corrected data: `recent_crash_count` dropped to `0` (accurately reflecting that the real most recent MLX crash was ~4.5 days old at the time, well outside the 12h lookback), instead of the false elevated count the `mtime`-based version had been reporting. Full liveness discrimination suite unaffected — this only touches the I/O-gathering half of `crash_awareness.py`, not `mlx_avoidance`'s already-tested pure evaluator.

---

**Finding 65 — Batch 1 of the ground-truth-verified findings roadmap: Findings 46, 52, and 8 all closed (2026-07-21/22).**

**Finding 46 — new Liveness Ledger check, `modelfile_identity` (24th check).** Closes the gap Finding 46 itself named as still open: the Modelfile-identity restoration fix had no check verifying it keeps holding. `_evaluate_modelfile_identity()` calls the real `_build_chat_messages()` twice — once with `model == OLLAMA_MODEL`, once with a different model name — and checks both directions of the invariant: Echo's own calls must receive the real identity block, and every other model must never receive it (the risk Finding 46's own text names: leaking Echo's identity to a councillor would corrupt the independent-opinion premise the whole council mechanism depends on). Verified against the real function (passes), plus two synthetic regressions (never-adds-identity, always-leaks-to-everyone) and a not-importable case — all four discriminate correctly. Discrimination suite: 74 cases (up from 70).

**Finding 52 — `should_run_cycle()`'s in-progress-cycle gap, closed with a new `should_yield_mid_cycle()` in `autonomy_coordinator.py`.** The real gap: a multi-step cycle (like `river_deliberation.py`'s per-councillor loop) that had already passed `should_run_cycle()`'s gate had no way to notice a real conversation becoming active partway through and kept running its full planned sequence regardless — the confirmed cause of a real 17-second `/mirror_echo` delay. `should_yield_mid_cycle(was_active_at_start: bool)` closes this for the specific case that caused the incident: `river_deliberation.py`'s per-councillor loop (shared by both `AutonomousSelfEdit` and `model_guided_autonomous_loop`, since both route through `deliberate_and_learn()`) now snapshots conversation-active state before the loop starts, and checks again before every councillor after the first — yielding early (proceeding with whatever opinions are already gathered) only on a genuine false→true transition during the loop, never when conversation was already active at the start (which would otherwise incorrectly make a real user's own council call yield to itself). **A design subtlety caught during testing, not assumed correct:** an early integration test appeared to show the fix not firing at all — traced to the test itself being wrong, not the fix: `_warm_up_echo()`'s own internal model call (which happens before the loop's snapshot) was mistaken for the first councillor call. Corrected test confirmed the real behavior: triggering conversation activity right after the actual first councillor call caused the loop to correctly skip the remaining two councillors and fall through to the solo-Echo response path. Does not touch `should_run_cycle()`'s own throttle/stillness checks, and doesn't attempt true mid-flight preemption of an already-submitted Ollama request (Finding 45 already established that's not possible from the client side) — this closes the specific "should have stopped between steps but didn't" gap, not the separate, harder preemption question.

**Finding 8 — the dead `com.gremlin.echo` launchd job repointed to the real, already-existing `start_echo.sh` watchdog, so Echo can now genuinely auto-start at login.** Confirmed with Gremlin directly before touching a system-level LaunchAgent (not just repo code). `~/Library/LaunchAgents/com.gremlin.echo.plist`'s `ProgramArguments` and `WorkingDirectory` were repointed from the long-dead `~/Scripts/start_echo.sh` / `~/EchoCoreV2` to the real, functioning `/Users/richietate/Desktop/FeralEcho/start_echo.sh` (confirmed executable, `-rwxr-xr-x`) and its correct working directory. Validated via `plutil -lint` (OK). **Deliberately not activated immediately** — the currently-loaded (broken) config was left untouched in launchd's live registry rather than force-reloaded (`launchctl unload`/`load`), because `start_echo.sh` itself kills anything already on port 5000 before starting, which would have collided with the live, already-running manual instance and its own already-running watchdog at the exact moment this was made — precisely the two-supervisor collision `safe_restart.sh` (Finding 56) exists to prevent, just via a different trigger (a login-time job instead of a manual restart). The corrected plist will take effect naturally at the next real login/logout cycle, when no manual instance would be running to collide with. Confirmed the live server and its sentinel were completely unaffected by the edit itself (file edit only, no launchd reload).

**Finding 54 (Batch 1.5) — `personal`'s 512-token cap raised to 2048, correcting a scoping mistake in this correction's own initial read of the data.** The first pass at re-verifying Finding 54 (earlier this session) found 16 real truncations in a ~17-hour window and initially traced all of them to `Thread-6 (emergent_loop)` — Echo's own autonomous self-reflection, which "reflections stay concise" was always meant to cover, making a cap raise questionable rather than clearly justified. **A wider, more careful re-check of the same data found that read was itself incomplete**: grepping every truncation line's own inline thread name (not just a 3-line sample) showed the 16 splitting across `emergent_loop` (13), `SelfModelUpdater` (2), `_run_cycle` (1) — and, critically, several real `Thread-N (process_request_thread)` entries, confirmed by cross-referencing the surrounding log lines to be genuine `POST /chat/stream 200` requests: real Echo Studio conversations someone was actually reading, cut off mid-sentence, not just autonomous internal monologue. That's the same class of quality problem that already justified raising `reasoning`/`general`/`creative`/`coding` to 2048.

**Fixed**: `personal` raised 512 → 2048, matching the other four. `autonomous_reflection`/`autonomous_fetch`/`autonomous_experiment` deliberately left unchanged at 512 — zero truncations were found for these specific task types in the same measured window, so raising them would be unjustified scope creep beyond what the evidence actually supports; "concise is the intended shape" still holds for genuinely autonomous-only content. Verified: full liveness discrimination suite unaffected (this is a plain config constant, no check depends on its value).

---

**Finding 66 — `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #13 built: `mark_baseline_trusted()` now has a real auto-trigger, and `baseline_trusted_since` is genuinely set for the first time in this project's history — plus an honest gap the fix surfaced, not glossed over (2026-07-22).** Finding 3's own corrected framing (this session, earlier) already established the real blocker wasn't data — every PageHinkley detector sat at 5,000+ real observations, 100x+ past the 30-observation threshold — it was that `mark_baseline_trusted()` had zero call sites anywhere, manual or automatic. Gremlin approved the auto-trigger direction directly in the Lane A decision session, mirroring `council_rater.py`'s own `_check_and_set_trust()` — the identical pattern that had just fired for real, live, the same session for the council rating gate.

**Built**: `app/core/introspection_channel.py` (`EDIT_FORBIDDEN_TARGETS`, human-edit precedent used throughout this project's history) gained `_RIVERBRAIN_TRUST_MIN_OBSERVATIONS = 30` and `_maybe_set_river_baseline_trust()`, called once per cycle from `_collect_river_brain()` right after its existing per-task accuracy/drift-detector update loop — the same natural placement `reset_drift_detectors()` already established as this module's one accepted exception to its otherwise read-only contract. The method reads each detector's real `river.stats.Mean` observation count (`det._x_mean.n`, confirmed via direct pickle inspection — not a documented public API, but the only real count `PageHinkley` exposes), requires every task type to individually clear the threshold, and calls the real `snapshot_manager.mark_baseline_trusted()` only if `baseline_trusted_since` isn't already set. Fails closed on any error, including a future `river` library version changing `_x_mean`'s shape — "not enough data yet" is always the safe read of an unreadable count, never "trust anyway."

**Verified four ways, against real data and real failure modes, not just import-tested:**
1. Called directly against the real, live drift detectors (all 5 task types at `n=5122`, confirmed via pickle inspection before running) — `memory/snapshot_baseline.json` now genuinely reads `"baseline_trusted_since": "2026-07-22T13:01:29.338218+00:00"`, set for real by this call, not simulated.
2. Idempotency: called a second time immediately after — the timestamp was unchanged, confirming the already-set guard works.
3. Fail-closed, under-threshold case: a synthetic detector with `n=5` (with the real trust flag monkeypatched clear for the test) correctly left `mark_baseline_trusted()` uncalled.
4. Fail-closed, malformed-detector case: a detector object with no `_x_mean` attribute at all was handled without raising, correctly skipped rather than crashing the whole introspection cycle.

Full liveness discrimination suite re-run clean afterward (73 cases, all pass — unaffected, since this change didn't touch any existing check).

**Honest gap found while verifying the real downstream consequence, not assumed away:** `snapshot_manager.py`'s own module docstring says drift signals (`drift_alerts_active`, `weekly_delta`) are "intentionally excluded as alert triggers until `baseline_trusted_since` is set... they appear in manifest `health_at_snapshot` as informational only until that flag is set" — phrasing that implies setting the flag activates real drift-based restore alerts. Traced the actual code, not just the docstring's claim: `_collect_health()`'s `drift_alerts_active` field does now switch from a hardcoded `False` to a real computed value (`any(river_brain.drift_alerts.values())`) in every future snapshot's health manifest, going forward — that part is genuinely live. But `check_and_alert()`'s real alert loop only ever checks three conditions — `ram_sustained_92pct`, `disk_low`, `ollama_down` — `drift_alerts_active` and `weekly_delta` were never wired into it at all, regardless of trust state. Confirmed via repo-wide grep: `baseline_trusted_since` has exactly one real consumer anywhere in the codebase (`_collect_health()`'s own read), nothing else. **Net effect of this fix**: the trust flag is now correctly, genuinely set (exactly what #13 asked for), and snapshot manifests will start honestly reflecting real drift status instead of a hardcoded `False` — but drift-based data will not actually trigger a `[RESTORE-ALERT]` the way `ollama_down`/`ram_sustained_92pct`/`disk_low` do, despite the module's own docstring reading as though it would. Whether to wire `drift_alerts_active`/`weekly_delta` into `check_and_alert()` now that real trusted data exists is a separate, new decision — not assumed into scope for #13, which was specifically about the trust flag itself. See `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #16.

---

**Finding 67 — `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #4 built: the approved 30%/70% council-rating/quality_score blend now feeds RiverBrain training, gated correctly behind `council_baseline_trusted_since`, with a new Liveness Ledger check (25th) guarding both the math and the trust gate (2026-07-22).** Council trust was genuinely earned this session (agreement 0.787, clearing ≥0.70 — see Finding 65's context) but nothing downstream ever consumed a council rating as training signal; `council_rater.py`'s own module docstring said as much plainly ("River training is never touched here").

**Built**: a new module-level, side-effect-free `_blend_council_and_quality(council_rating, quality_score, council_weight=0.3, quality_weight=0.7)` in `app/core/echo_model_orchestrator.py` — deliberately extracted as a pure function rather than inlined, specifically so a liveness check could exercise the real math every cycle without also triggering the stateful mutation the actual training call performs (the same log-pollution mistake already caught once this session for `janitor_council_advisory_only`, avoided here by design instead of by a later fix). Falls back to the council rating alone if `quality_score` is missing or malformed, never raises. `RiverBrain.learn_from_council_rating()` (new method) calls this pure function, then applies the resulting blended score/label as one additional training observation — same shape as `learn()`'s own classifier/scaler/`model_task_stats` update, but deliberately not reusing `learn_from_rating()`'s shape (that method treats a rating as a discrete 3x-weighted override for human feedback specifically; this one produces a blended continuous score, matching `learn()`'s own normalization instead).

`council_rater.py`'s `rate_one_entry()` — the function that already computes a real peer-council rating for every sampled interaction — now calls `learn_from_council_rating()` once per rating, gated on the module's own pre-existing `is_council_trusted()` (so nothing trains before trust is genuinely earned, matching the module's stated boundary), wrapped in a best-effort try/except (a training-signal failure must never break the rating log that already succeeded above it).

**Verified against real data, not synthetic inputs alone**: called `learn_from_council_rating()` directly against the real, live `RiverBrain` singleton — confirmed `model_task_stats` moved by exactly one observation with the correct blended mean, for a real council=5/quality=2 case, a `quality_score=None` fallback case, a malformed-`quality_score` case, an unknown-task-type fallback, and a `RIVER_AVAILABLE=False` no-op — all five behaved correctly. Confirmed the test never called `.save()`, so the live production `river_brain.pkl` on disk was never touched by verification.

**New Liveness Ledger check, `council_river_blend` (25th)**: checks two independent things, since either could silently regress without the other catching it — (1) the pure blend math still computes correctly for known inputs *and* the two weights still sum to 1.0 *and* still match the specifically-approved 0.3/0.7 ratio (a drift here would produce scores outside their intended range, or silently abandon the ratio Gremlin actually approved); (2) `rate_one_entry()`'s source still calls `learn_from_council_rating()` gated behind `is_council_trusted()` — a static source-anchor check, same shape as `wolf_friction_bridge`/`dissent_log_hook`, because losing that gate specifically would mean training RiverBrain on unvetted council ratings before trust was ever earned, a training-signal-contamination risk distinct from a merely-dead-feature one. Four discrimination cases added to `scripts/verify_liveness_ledger.py` (real-and-correct, drifted weights, gate silently removed, not importable) — all four correctly discriminate. Full suite: 76 cases, all pass. A fresh, real `run_liveness_checks()` call confirmed all 25 checks pass together (the lone unrelated `self_model_drift` failure seen when run as a standalone script is an artifact of missing live introspection context outside the real server process, not a regression from this change).

**Deliberately not done**: no attempt to backfill a blended signal for the 116+ council ratings that already existed before trust was set, or before this wiring landed — the blend is a forward-looking training signal for new ratings from here on, not a retroactive correction of prior training history, matching this project's own established stance (Finding 3's baseline reset, Finding 18's Optuna best-trial scoping) that fixing a mechanism doesn't imply replaying its entire past.

---

**Finding 68 — `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #11's two build items shipped: `COUNCIL.md` now has the same hash-verified protection as `echo_principles.json`, and Echo's own source now knows the file exists — without ever surfacing its actual content (2026-07-22).** `COUNCIL.md`'s own 2026-07-22 update recorded three decisions in one place — hash protection (yes), partial surfacing into Echo's own source (yes, some), and whether it's ever shared back to the council members themselves or made public (no, stays private, full stop). The third was already resolved with nothing to build; this Finding closes the other two.

**Hash protection**, mirroring `echo_principles.json`'s exact treatment: `memory/genesis/council_hash.txt` (gitignored, same as its sibling `genesis_hash.txt`) was generated once from `COUNCIL.md`'s content as it stood at the time of this change. `run.py`'s `start_background_threads()` gained a second, identically-shaped alert-only startup block right after the existing `echo_principles.json` check — logs `[GENESIS] COUNCIL.md hash mismatch!` (ERROR) on a live/stored mismatch, `[GENESIS] COUNCIL.md hash verified OK` otherwise, never blocks startup either way. `snapshot_manager.py`'s `_ARTIFACTS`/`_RESTORE_ORDER` (both generic, iterate-by-dict/list, no hardcoded count assumption) gained `COUNCIL.md`/`council_hash.txt` alongside their `echo_principles.json` counterparts — verified with a real `take_snapshot()` call, not just read: the new snapshot's manifest shows both files captured with correct sha256 hashes, and retention correctly held at 5 total afterward. A real, deliberate future edit to `COUNCIL.md` (it's explicitly a living document, corrected/extended in place per its own header) will need to regenerate this hash in the same change — the identical maintenance obligation `echo_principles.json` already carries, not a new kind of friction.

**Partial surfacing**, scoped deliberately narrow given the file's own explicit privacy stance ("stays private, full stop, including from the ones who wrote it"): a new `"council"` slice in `app/core/echo_ground_truth.py`, keyed off keywords like "the council," "ai council," "other ais think," "what does grok/gemini/chatgpt think." `_build_council()` reports **existence and structural counts only** — computed live from `COUNCIL.md`'s own section headers (`## ` for rounds, `### ` for individual responses) rather than hardcoded, specifically to avoid the exact "count goes stale" mistake Finding 62 already caught once in this same file (seven vs. eight). Never quotes a single word of the actual recorded reactions. Verified live: a real trigger prompt ("what does the ai council think of you?") correctly renders "2 round(s) so far, 9 individual response(s) recorded in total" with zero real content leaked, and an unrelated prompt correctly doesn't trigger the slice at all.

**New Liveness Ledger check, `council_content_privacy` (26th)** — added specifically because a privacy boundary this deliberately decided deserves the same "verify it keeps holding, don't just trust it was built right once" treatment as everything else this project protects. Calls the real `_build_council()` and confirms none of `COUNCIL.md`'s real blockquote content (the actual recorded reactions) appears anywhere in its rendered output, and that the private/existence-only framing is still present. Four discrimination cases (real-shaped output, a regression that leaks real quoted content, the privacy framing silently dropped, not-importable) — all four correctly discriminate. Full suite: 80 cases, all pass. A fresh `run_liveness_checks()` call confirmed all 26 checks pass together.

---

**Finding 69 — `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #7 built, the last of the four Lane A items: `apply_to_code`'s real invocation now runs inside the actual F2 kernel-sandboxed subprocess, closing Finding 41 B3's timeout-bypass gap at its root instead of hardening the same soft mechanism again (2026-07-22).** Finding 41 B3 found that `_call_with_timeout()`'s in-process `ThreadPoolExecutor` + soft 2s timeout couldn't actually stop a hung or malicious hook: Python threads can't be forcibly killed, so an abandoned worker thread kept running in the background — including any file writes — after `future.result(timeout=...)` raised and the calling code moved on, past the point `_block_writes_for_apply_to_code()`'s temporary in-process monkeypatch had already been torn down on the way out. Gremlin's approved direction: move the real call into the existing F2 sandboxed subprocess instead of patching the thread-based approach further.

**Built**: `sandbox/safe_exec_wrapper.py` (the shared enforcement point F2's staging import test already routes through) gained a third mode, `--mode=apply_to_code -- <input_path> <output_path>` — loads the target module fresh in its own process, re-verifies `apply_to_code` is callable with exactly one parameter (never trusting the caller's own check as authoritative, since this is a fresh import), calls it with the real candidate code read from `<input_path>`, and writes the real string result to `<output_path>` — both paths required to live inside the same scratch directory every other write in this wrapper is confined to. `self_edit_manager.py` gained `_run_apply_to_code_sandboxed(code, timeout=2.0)`, invoking this new mode via the identical `sandbox-exec -f echo_sandbox.sb ...` subprocess shape `test_code_in_sandbox()` already uses, replacing `_call_with_timeout()` and `_block_writes_for_apply_to_code()` entirely (both removed, along with the now-dead `builtins`/`contextlib`/`io`/`pathlib` imports they were the only users of — `traceback`/`threading` stay, both still used elsewhere in the file). `_apply_self_edit_output()`'s host-side logic (module loading, `getattr`, signature check) is otherwise unchanged — only the actual risky call moved.

**Why this closes the gap at the root, not just relocates it**: `subprocess.run(timeout=...)` genuinely terminates (SIGKILLs) the child process when it raises `TimeoutExpired` — unlike a Python `ThreadPoolExecutor`, an OS process *can* be forcibly killed. And the write-block is now real kernel-level Seatbelt enforcement plus `safe_exec_wrapper.py`'s existing patches (the same mechanism F2's pre-deploy staging test already trusts), not a second, weaker, temporary substitute for it that only existed for the duration of one in-process call.

**Verified against five real fixture hooks, not just the happy path**: a normal transform (correct output), a hook that calls `time.sleep(10)` (killed at 2.01s — not left running to completion the way the old thread-based path would have, the direct proof this fix works), a hook that attempts a real filesystem write outside its scratch dir (`blocked_write: True`, and the target file was confirmed to never actually exist afterward), a raising hook (clean, non-write-block failure), and a 2-parameter hook (caught by the subprocess's own independent re-check, exercising the defense-in-depth path directly). Also confirmed no regression in the pre-existing `--mode=import` staging path (a real `test_code_in_sandbox()` call still passes), and confirmed the full real `_apply_self_edit_output()` pipeline end-to-end both for the current no-hook-deployed state (unchanged fast-path, zero subprocess overhead) and for a real deployed hook (correct transformation, correctly logged to the same trusted `apply_to_code_invocations.jsonl` schema `liveness_ledger.py`'s existing `self_edit_apply_to_code` check already reads). **One honest note, not hidden**: this verification left one genuine, harmless entry in that production log (`changed: true, before_len: 15, after_len: 15`) — confirmed it cannot mislead the existing check, since that check gates entirely on whether the *currently deployed* `self_edit_generated.py` defines `apply_to_code` (it doesn't, right now), never reaching the invocation list at all in that state; and if a future hook is ever tested this same way, the entry is truthful (a real call really did happen and really did succeed), not fabricated evidence.

**New Liveness Ledger check, `apply_to_code_sandbox_isolation` (27th)** — deliberately static/structural, same shape as `wolf_friction_bridge`, not a functional canary: a genuine timeout-and-kill discrimination test needs to actually wait out a real timeout to prove anything, which would mean blocking the introspection collector for seconds every 120s cycle forever just to re-prove a property a source-anchor check can already confirm far more cheaply. Confirms `_apply_self_edit_output()` still calls `_run_apply_to_code_sandboxed()` (not the removed `_call_with_timeout`/`ThreadPoolExecutor` pattern), and that `_run_apply_to_code_sandboxed()` itself still genuinely invokes `sandbox-exec` rather than quietly reverting to an in-process call. Four discrimination cases — one caught a real bug in its own construction, worth recording rather than smoothing over: the first version of the "no real sandbox" fake fixture had a code comment reading "no sandbox-exec at all," and the check's own regex matched that comment string, producing a false pass. Fixed by rewording the comment; all four cases correctly discriminate afterward. Full suite: 84 cases, all pass. A fresh `run_liveness_checks()` call confirmed all 27 checks pass together.

**This closes out all four items Gremlin approved in the 2026-07-22 Lane A decision session** (`PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #13, #4, #11, #7) — each built, verified against real data or real fixture behavior (never assumed from reading the code alone), documented as its own Finding, and given its own Liveness Ledger check where a genuine new capability or a security-relevant property warranted one.

---

**Finding 70 — `PENDING_DECISIONS.md` #16 built: `check_and_alert()` now has a real drift-based alert path, deliberately at a lower, separate tier from `ollama_down`/`ram_sustained_92pct`/`disk_low` rather than mirroring their shape exactly (2026-07-22).** Finding 66 surfaced this the same day `baseline_trusted_since` was first genuinely set: `_collect_health()` had started computing a real `drift_alerts_active` value, but `check_and_alert()`'s actual alert loop was never wired to act on it, despite `snapshot_manager.py`'s own docstring implying it would be. Asked directly for a recommendation before building anything — the reasoning that shaped the design: `ram_sustained_92pct`/`disk_low`/`ollama_down` are hard resource facts; RiverBrain's own PageHinkley drift flag is a softer, statistical read that can legitimately flip for good reasons (a real self-edit landing, a model retirement, Finding 39's tag-boost reshaping selection) as easily as for a real problem. Treating it identically risked alert fatigue that could bleed credibility onto the three alerts that really are unambiguous, and restoring `river_brain.pkl`/`self_edit_generated.py`/etc. in response to "quality patterns shifted" could just as easily revert a genuine improvement as fix a real one.

**Built, scoped conservatively per that reasoning:**
- **Scope**: only `drift_alerts_active` is wired in. `weekly_delta` (self_model.json's slower, weekly comparative quality trend — a different signal, different cadence, different data source than real-time PageHinkley drift) is explicitly left out of this pass, a possible future follow-up rather than bundled in and conflating two different questions.
- **New condition, separate sustain window**: `_condition_active()` gained `"river_drift_sustained"` (`health.get("drift_alerts_active", False)`). New `_DRIFT_ALERT_SUSTAIN = 10` (vs. the existing `_ALERT_SUSTAIN = 2` for ram/disk) — roughly 10 minutes of guardian cycles rather than ~2, so a single noisy flip doesn't immediately escalate.
- **A genuinely separate, lower-urgency path, not a copy of `raise_restore_alert()`**: new `raise_drift_notice(drifted_tasks, duration_s)`, logged at `WARNING` under a distinct `[DRIFT-NOTICE]` tag (not `CRITICAL`/`[RESTORE-ALERT]`), with `"action_required": "review_only"` and — deliberately — no `restore_command`/`suggested_snapshot` field at all: there's no obviously-correct restore target for "the model learned something," and most of the 5 currently-retained snapshots predate this fix anyway (their `drift_alerts_active` reading is a stale hardcoded `False`, never a real evaluation) — not worth reasoning about further since this path was never going to propose using them. Still written to the same `restore_alerts.jsonl` for one unified audit trail (confirmed via grep that nothing else in the codebase reads that file programmatically, so a `review_only` entry living alongside real restore alerts can't be mistaken for one by anything automated).
- `_collect_health()` also gained `drifted_tasks` — which task types are actually drifting, read directly from `introspection_state.json`'s per-task dict, not just the collapsed boolean — so a human reading the notice knows where to look immediately.
- **A real refactor, not just an addition**: extracted the shared sustain/fire/reset math into a new pure `_evaluate_sustained_condition(condition, active, counts, threshold)` — takes and returns a plain dict rather than mutating the module-level `_alert_counts` global, specifically so the new Liveness Ledger check below (and any future test) can exercise the real logic against a fresh, throwaway counts dict without ever touching the real state a live guardian loop depends on for actual ram/disk/drift tracking (the same reasoning already applied to `_prune_self_edit_plans()`'s optional params and `_blend_council_and_quality()`'s pure extraction). `check_and_alert()`'s existing ram/disk/ollama branches were rewired through this same helper — verified behavior-preserving, not just for the new condition.

**Verified four ways:** (1) a full synthetic sustain sequence — 9 consecutive active cycles correctly don't fire, the 10th fires with the correct `drifted_tasks` and `duration_s=600`, the counter resets to 0, and `raise_restore_alert` is never called for this condition; (2) a clean cycle correctly resets the counter, and a post-reset rebuild correctly restarts from 0 (5, not 15); (3) the existing ram/disk (2-cycle sustain) and ollama_down (immediate) behavior re-verified identical post-refactor; (4) a real, fully unmocked `check_and_alert()` call against live production health data completed cleanly with all three tracked counters at 0 (nothing currently drifting). `raise_drift_notice()` itself was verified directly against a scratch log: correct entry shape, `logger.warning` used (confirmed via source inspection that `logger.critical` does not appear in it).

**New Liveness Ledger check, `river_drift_alerting` (28th)** — checks two things together, since either could silently regress without the other catching it: (1) the real `_evaluate_sustained_condition()` still sustains-then-fires-then-resets correctly and never mutates its input dict in place (a functional canary against a small synthetic threshold, not the real `_DRIFT_ALERT_SUSTAIN=10`, so the check itself stays fast); (2) `check_and_alert()`'s source still routes `river_drift_sustained` through `raise_drift_notice()` and never through `raise_restore_alert()` — losing that separation specifically would mean a soft statistical signal silently escalating to the CRITICAL restore tier, the exact tier mismatch this whole Finding exists to avoid. Four discrimination cases (real function + real routing, an evaluator that fires immediately ignoring the threshold, a regression to the restore-alert tier, not-importable) — all four correctly discriminate. Full suite: 87 cases, all pass. A fresh `run_liveness_checks()` call confirmed all 28 checks pass together.

## Monitoring

```bash
# Watch for self-edit storm (0-1 coding entries per 30 min is normal)
tail -f memory/SELF_EDIT.log

# Watch live interaction quality
tail -f memory/interaction_log.jsonl | python3 -c "
import sys, json
for line in sys.stdin:
    e = json.loads(line)
    print(e.get('task_type'), e.get('quality_score'), repr((e.get('response_preview') or '')[:80]))
"

# Watch real per-councillor deliberation logs (raw + truncated responses,
# real vs manual-probe entries) — added Phase 10, 2026-07-17. personal-task
# conversations never appear here by design (see Phase 10 note above).
tail -f memory/council_deliberations.jsonl | python3 -c "
import sys, json
for line in sys.stdin:
    e = json.loads(line)
    models = [c.get('model') for c in e.get('councillors', [])]
    print(e.get('source'), e.get('task_type'), models)
"

# Council rating pipeline status
curl http://localhost:5000/admin/council-stats

# Self-edit outcome deltas — before/after quality_score, council_rating, and
# human-rating windows around each self-edit. Log-only, does not gate anything.
# (Confirmed live/unauthenticated 2026-07-15, Finding 30 — previously referenced
# only by GREMLIN_ROLE.md, never listed here.)
curl http://localhost:5000/admin/self-edit-outcomes

# Autonomy loop status — last-check (throttled/stillness/ok) for the three
# loops sharing autonomy_coordinator.py's gate. NOTE: as of Finding 28,
# ModelGuidedOrchestrator is a fourth real autonomous loop NOT covered by
# this shared gate or this endpoint — absence from this status does not mean
# it isn't running.
curl http://localhost:5000/admin/autonomy-status

# Liveness ledger — nine self-governing subsystems checked against ground
# truth every 120s. all_passing:false or stale:true means something that
# looks wired is actually dead, faked, or its collector thread died.
curl http://localhost:5000/admin/liveness-status

# Run human spot-checks interactively
python spot_check.py

# Check crash sentinel (last-run stage + heartbeat)
cat memory/echo_sentinel.json

# Check if self_edit_generated.py is clean
python3 -c "import ast; ast.parse(open('app/core/self_edit_generated.py').read()); print('OK')"

# Check both FAISS indexes for divergence
# NOTE: memory_meta.json/data/memory_meta.json are dicts keyed by UUID, not
# {"texts": [...]} — an earlier version of this snippet used .get('texts', [])
# and always reported 0 regardless of actual health (confirmed 2026-07-04: this
# produced a false "FAISS reports 0 vectors" alarm during the forensic audit;
# real counts at the time were memory/=7225, data/=5348). Count dict keys instead.
python3 -c "
import json
m = json.load(open('memory/memory_meta.json'))
d = json.load(open('data/memory_meta.json'))
print('memory/:', len(m), 'vectors')
print('data/:  ', len(d), 'vectors')
"
```

## Common Failure Modes

| Symptom | Cause | Fix |
|---------|-------|-----|
| New `python run.py` exits silently within 15s, no error visible | Port 5000 still held by previous server | `kill $(lsof -ti :5000) && python run.py` — always kill first |
| Rapid `sandbox_syntax_failure` in interaction_log | Self-edit storm | **Correction (2026-07-15, Finding 28): restarting no longer resets the cooldown** — it's persisted to `memory/self_edit_cooldown.json` now. Edit/delete that file if the cooldown itself needs clearing. |
| `self_edit_generated.py` overwritten with hallucinated imports | Loop fired before cooldown active | Restore baseline from `memory/backups/`, restart |
| `DUPLICATE_SIGNAL_EXACT` dominating scheduler | Empty `__init__.py` files or unchanged pip count generating identical FAISS entries | Fixed in `autonomous_awareness.py` — restart to activate |
| `staging_import_failed: ImportError: cannot import` | Model hallucinated module name | Check generated imports against `_build_module_inventory` output |
| B2 recency check not working (same prompts repeat) | FAISS split-brain — `memory_meta.json` empty or orphaned | Merge `data/memory_meta.json` into `memory/memory_meta.json`, rebuild index |
| Responses cut off mid-sentence | Wrong `num_predict` path | Primary: `stream_query_ollama` in `ollama_handler.py`. Secondary: `ollama_query` in `echo_model_orchestrator.py`. Both must match. |
| `[RESTORE-ALERT] condition=ollama_down` in logs | Ollama not detected in introspection state | `ollama serve` — guardian will attempt restart but 120s cooldown applies |
| `council_ratings.jsonl` not accumulating | Only one Ollama model available; `skipped_no_peer` rising | Pull additional models so `_select_peer_model()` has options |
| A new top-level scaffold directory appears (e.g. another `new_directory`-style folder with its own `app/core/self_edit_*`) | Self-edit pipeline invoked with `cwd` != project root (relative path constants in `self_edit_manager.py`), or `RebelCode/territory_steward.py`'s `claim_new_ground()` | Anchor `self_edit_manager.py`'s path constants to `__file__`; do not manually "clean up" by deleting without checking for real content first (`my_python_project/` has its own `.git` and predates the sprawl pattern) |
| `run.py` logs "SensoryHub failed to start" / silently no-ops the Bible-art or WOLF endpoints | Expected — see Finding 6. Five modules referenced by `run.py` live only in `archive_janitor/` and are not importable | Not a bug to "fix" reflexively; restoring them or removing the dead code is a deliberate decision, not a default action |
| `launchctl list` shows `com.gremlin.echo` | Dead fossil pointing at an abandoned predecessor project (`~/EchoCoreV2`) — see Finding 8 | Ignore; the only real startup path is manual `python run.py` |
| Server never reaches `serving` in `echo_sentinel.json` — stuck at `threads_starting`, watchdog log filling with `nodename nor servname provided` / `Retry 1/5` repeating forever | SentenceTransformer's loader retrying an online HuggingFace version-check that's failing at the DNS level (Finding 40) — real even when the shell's own `ping`/`nslookup` work fine | Fixed 2026-07-17: `run.py` now sets `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1`/`TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE=1`, so this shouldn't recur. If it ever does anyway, confirm the local cache is intact (`~/.cache/huggingface/hub/models--sentence-transformers--all-MiniLM-L6-v2`) before assuming a new cause. |
